EU leaders hand Ukraine peace talks to France, Germany, UK, Italy, Poland plus Brussels – instead of a single envoy

Support us <https://email.mgeu.euobserver.com/c/eJyEkc9OxCAQh58Gbm4KtEAPHEw2fQ3Cn2mLFtpQuurbGzarqxsTrzN8M_P7sKa4WQevuBASuzUVeC8KjtXukC-Qn7wJy8eJtrIXBHvVOeACgyKibyVpeE_xrKS1jrcd6aznwrey8dA3QgpiwfmRGhxUhrjpaMICWXtKR2K51N9DCGqbOMFxui8-uTXil9XebiN4UXMp247YM6IDooNxbj1SeUAQHSJEC3mfw4bYkEvUbgnuFbHzaD0AdYAor-V9PbIDxM6PaW_9CD4csfbr3beiM3EzYUq_MB3XnEKa9LF5U0ATTgjlX0R1mgpi56viOkvvkDzkGo0CGzvp3V0FzirB275AKZBJy1iP2maq2FXJz8_5ey8uELfFFPj_5UXRzwAAAP__7Nutew>

Good morning.

Russian elites are back on the menu in EU sanctions, but Ukraine peace talks seem to be headed toward another anticlimax. 137 individuals and entities are to be added to an existing EU blacklist of over 4,600 names.

The new sanctions also name 49 Russian banks and a leading insurance firm, Balance Insurance. And they strike at global enablers of Russia's oil trade in the Middle East and further afield.

The upcoming blacklist is to include 13 oil trade and shipping firms in the UAE, three in China, one in the Seychelles, and one in Singapore, said to be helping Russian president Vladimir Putin keep oil dollars flowing.

But if this was meant to show strength, it could have been stronger...

Kung Fu & Tax Deductions: How Chinese Buddhism Became Dominant

A History of Mankind (422)

David Roman

Nov 16

Preview

READ IN APP

The typically Semitic idea of an all-powerful, all-watchful, fairly impersonal and indescribable God that superseded all others and required undivided allegiance got stranded in the Central Asian deserts and never quite made any impact in China. There, learned elites accustomed to multiple ways of worship assessed such idea as a curious, un-needed western notion that didn’t quite hold up to scrutiny...

-Nvidia: Gerüchte über ein Comeback des Gründers

-SPD: Vorwarts, Genossen, in die Vergangenheit

-SpaceX: Das Wunder von Texas

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uLqWp0h5ppwoxC9XGjlgsj5QVvizEbx0YXbVbNcSRrO-Ao65adAn3f_XikW_c-fsSykK55P3Qmbq72O-8T24i13vEl6eACrnw

Ex-Nvidia-Chef Jensen Huang: Kommt die Zukunft aus der Vergangenheit? (Foto: Soeren Stache/dpa)

Guten Morgen, liebe Leserin, lieber Leser,im Nvidia-Skandal gedeihen Gerüchte über eine spektakulare Kehrtwende: Wie die „Financial Times“ gestern Abend berichtete, arbeitet Mitgründer und Ex-CEO Jensen Huang an einem Comeback. Huang war am Donnerstag abgetreten, nachdem eine Affare mit einer mutmaßlichen chinesischen Spionin sowie mindestens ein weiteres Verhaltnis mit einer Untergebenen bekannt geworden waren.

Laut „FT“ sammelt ein Unterstützer Führungskrafte aus der Technologiebranche, um sich für Huang starkzumachen. Schon kurz nach dem Rücktritt startete die Online-Petition https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uMP5_pRsr_u-nk2o0eD4oPXonmyWxDQVz3NdeUcuK09FbGqc0e9WEK6W5S9E-z3X_QTWCtmrTicIfdg4odpRiRWn6Y-kc3Gcg „Stand with Jensen“ , ein offener Brief an den Verwaltungsrat. Sie zahlte heute Nacht etwa 165.000 Unterschriften, davon über 8000 von Nvidia-Mitarbeitenden, was aber nicht überprüfbar ist. Auch Silicon-Valley-Großen wie Elon Musk und Epic-Games-Chef Tim Sweeney warben für Huang.

Dass die Rückkehr die einfachste Alternative ware, liegt angesichts der holprigen Nachfolgersuche des Nvidia-Boards nahe. Ob sie aber gut ware? https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uNXXLulKvOTQpQQLokyjmfzGcnojDmEWS5bdxQCkq2g8yytlAcvDmuoAWaAv9Il1fHVNL2U_ZDyhgVcT4sX0T9JIED7MZAVDY Unser US-Chef Felix Holtermann , der Nvidia seit Jahren beobachtet, sieht Huang jedenfalls kritisch. Er schreibt in einer Chronologie von Huangs Absturz:

Mehrere aktuelle und ehemalige Mitarbeiter berichten dem Handelsblatt, wie sehr sich die Stimmung im Konzern verschlechtert hat. Huang trete zunehmend herrisch auf.

Zentrale Kriterien für die Comeback-Option dürften zwei sein:

- Die China-Connection: Wird dem Nvidia-Gründer im Zuge seiner Affare mit Alice Huang, einer früheren Managerin des singapurischen Unternehmens Megaspeed, die Weitergabe sensibler Informationen nachgewiesen? US-Geheimdienste verdachtigen die Frau, für China spioniert zu haben. Megaspeed soll sie bereits im August 2025 verlassen haben. - Die politische Stimmung: Drei Ausschüsse des Reprasentantenhauses wollen Huang vorladen. Auch Finanzminister Scott Bessent verscharfte am Sonntag den Ton: „Nvidia braucht einen dramatischen Umbruch.“ Allerdings hat auch Washington kein Interesse daran, dass Nvidia als Rückgrat des KI-Booms in eine existenzielle Krise rutscht.

Ohne Vorbild ware ein Comeback nicht. Beim KI-Pionier OpenAI entließ das Board vor fast genau drei Jahren Mitgründer Sam Altman. Nach fünf Tagen war er zurück. Und so gehort zur Ironie dieser Tage, dass ausgerechnet ein Unternehmen, das wie kaum ein zweites für Zukunft steht, diese in der eigenen Vergangenheit finden konnte.

SPD-Aspirant Hubertus Heil: Noch ein Niedersachse. (Foto: Imago)

SPD: Vorwarts, Genossen, in die Vergangenheit

Der frühere Generalsekretar und Bundesarbeitsminister Hubertus Heil will beim SPD-Parteitag Mitte Januar als Vorsitzender kandidieren. Sollte Parteichef Lars Klingbeil erneut antreten, liefe es auf eine Kampfabstimmung hinaus.

Heil kommt, wie alle Manner, die in der SPD Machtansprüche erheben, aus Niedersachsen. Politisch aber aus einer anderen Welt. So fallen in Heils Amtszeit als Arbeitsminister etwa die Erfindung des Bürgergelds, das Lieferkettengesetz oder die Ausweitung der Pflicht zur Arbeitszeiterfassung. Ideen, die etwas ganz Seltenes geschafft haben: Wirtschaft und heutige SPD-Führung halten sie übereinstimmend für falsch.

Ahnlich segensreich fallt Heils operative Arbeit für die SPD aus: Er verantwortete als Generalsekretar den Bundestagswahlkampf 2009 und als Interims-Generalsekretar die letzte Phase des 2017er-Wahlkampfs – das waren bis zur jüngsten Wahl jene mit den schlechtesten SPD-Ergebnissen. Immerhin einen erfreulichen Neuigkeitswert hat die Heil-Kandidatur: Bei den letzten Parteivorsitzenden waren sich in der SPD immer im Nachhinein alle einig, die Personalie sei keine gute Idee gewesen. Bei Heil weiß man es schon vorher.

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uS0HiHmP4AKUaLgs247__A4jx3tMvdK1L9uNbis_8p5ppKyuOeaQ5YdUi3shztXxS26qAapTgTOO-XcWLAZnC6Gjz20HFbvlQ Jetzt Artikel lesen...

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uTuWbskHQs_Poxk9V4JeZTlrKSo8acQ21KeWdmI-94B804MyqXJMjCLILJ5E7aa22M074VREFUglYC5x4NCTQUELitBV9YC3s

Finanzminister Klingbeil: Bisschen weniger Papier wagen. (Foto: Kay Nietfeld/dpa)

Steuern: Klingbeils Vereinfachungsplan

Mit einem Vereinfachungsgesetz will Finanzminister Klingbeil die Steuererklarung leichter machen. Die wesentlichen Ideen des Papiers, das noch in einem frühen Stadium, aber dennoch nicht sicher vor Einblicken unserer Berliner Chefreporter Martin Greive und Jan Hildebrand ist:

- Für Privathaushalte sollen Abfindungen bis 5000 Euro unter Auflagen steuerfrei werden, etwa wenn binnen vier Monaten ein neuer Job folgt. Nebeneinkommen bis 800 Euro sollen nicht mehr erklarungspflichtig sein. Die Steuerklassen III und V sollen, wie an dieser Stelle schon berichtet, ab 2030 entfallen. - Für Unternehmen soll der Wechsel von der Einkommen- zur Korperschaftsteuer einfacher werden. Das wird interessant, wenn wie geplant die Einkommensteuer für sehr hohe Einkommen steigt, die Korperschaftsteuer aber sinkt.

Für alle soll die digitale Abgabe der Steuererklarung Pflicht werden. Laut Entwurf sollen dann 95 statt bisher etwa 78 Prozent der Erklarungen elektronisch eingehen. Meine Prognose: Von den fünf Prozent, die das dann immer noch nicht tun, werden wir in den nachsten Tagen Protest in einer Lautstarke horen, als handele es sich um 90 Prozent.

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uUZ45P0b7Yz8QzxpsIb6g5_gWEBpPbsLWq_Q9RsdT-vz4udgU-joD4Bldf9He8eE__aQ4vrVmbLhByK8WxQJev932h2lv7034 Jetzt Artikel lesen...

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uV0FKxwi9LIUT_hchyQkNLs_UVuw3wCgAy89addoaPmJFsMg3r5kRLz0QOVMRfsdXTE9ccFpJaNy4jiJXl8vpNiHukwf5YP_Q

Russische Holzpuppen mit den Konterfeis von Putin, Trump und Xi (v.r.n.l.). (Foto: AP)

Geopolitik: Drei Atommachte an einem Tisch

Am Donnerstag wollen sich Donald Trump, Xi Jinping und Wladimir Putin am Rande des Apec-Gipfels in Shenzhen treffen – das erste Dreiertreffen der führenden Atommachte USA, Russland und China überhaupt. Trump will offiziell über den Irankrieg und atomare Abrüstung reden.

In Europa wachst allerdings die Sorge vor einem „Jalta 2.0“, einem Ukraine-Deal ohne Ukrainer und Europaer. Bundeskanzler Friedrich Merz appelliert deswegen an Trump: „Bitte keine Uberraschungen.“ Der langjahrige China-Kammerprasident und heutige Berater Jorg Wuttke rechnet immerhin nicht damit, dass mehr als ein „Handschlag-Foto“ dabei herumkommt. Aus europaischer Sicht ware das schon ein Erfolg.

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uWeQRJ3QB8J7BeJJEsfs0CYJa9O86B0dnnPM-rTlrKNC79iASEzQb6wdPSCN_aAJ3609Ka5QP4-TlqUNuF217z7QxR99PFma0 Jetzt Artikel lesen...

https://click.redaktion.handelsblatt.com/?qs=ABl7ImQiOjUwNjIsInQiOjk5NzEsInMiOjd9AAcAAAAACXVD45uXNnolYuaSNGAc9-KLS6QEug82E3NkcHTXPHljjc4vQkOeAyZQQKc_UR2_bx-fLsn7hQb0aQ0SAoUGc5UUtnNCONJDPTEd5p8

KI-Managerin Dada: Ausweg aus der Dystopie. (Foto: Bloomberg)

KI: Wie Europa aufholen kann

Judith Dada ist ein optimistischer Mensch, sonst ware sie wohl als Tech-Investorin und neuerdings auch Co-Chefin des KI-Start-ups Langdock fehl am Platz. Und doch fiel sie zuletzt mit einer Dystopie auf: Mit mehreren KI-Experten veroffentlichte sie ein Szenario, nach dem Europa Anfang der 30er Jahre als Vasallenstaat der USA und Chinas endet. Zuversichtlich genug, dass es auch anders kommen kann, ist sie dennoch.

Dafür fordert Dada im Gesprach mit meiner Kollegin Larissa Holzki einen dramatischen Ausbau der Rechenkapazitaten in Europa. Die von der Bundesregierung geplante Vervierfachung bis 2030 reiche nicht. Zudem will sie ein europaisches 50-Milliarden-Euro-Programm, um „ein führendes europaisches KI-Labor aufzubauen“.

Generell brauche es drei Dinge:

Verbindliche Ausbauziele für Rechenleistung mit konkreten Kapitalzusagen, einen Staat, der Spitzen-KI in großem Stil selbst einkauft, und Regeln, die es Organisationen leichter machen, sich zu verandern, und zugleich unsere Bürger absichern und befahigen, gestarkt aus diesem Wandel hervorzugehen.

Frankfurt: Dem Himmel so nah

Zum Schluss schauen wir nach Frankfurt, wo die Büros schon immer etwas hoher sind als im Rest des Landes. Und künftig womoglich auch die Spazierwege. Die Stadt will aus einer Eisenbahnbrücke einen grünen Weg für Fußganger und Radfahrer machen. Vorbild ist die High Line in New York. Mainhattan wollte ja immer schon ein wenig wie New York sein. Mir ware zwar lieber, die Frankfurter hatten da eher bei Banken- oder Borsengroße gleichzuziehen versucht. Aber so eine Brücke kann ja ein Anfang auf dem Weg in die Zukunft sein.

Ich wünsche Ihnen jedenfalls einen zukunftstrachtigen Anfang der Woche.

Executive Briefing

Lee seeks ASEAN backing to turn South Korea’s trade ambitions into results

John Lee

President Lee Jae Myung will seek stronger economic ties with Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) leaders at a summit in Manila on Monday, as South Korea moves to implement its regional partnership agenda and expand cooperation in emerging industries. Lee will review progress under the Comprehensive Strategic Partnership (CSP) vision he introduced last year and seek ASEAN members’ support for its implementation, National Security Adviser Wi Sung-lac said Thursday.

The initiative builds on the 2026-2030 action plan adopted by Seoul and ASEAN in July 2025. Lee’s government has set a target of $300 billion in annual bilateral trade by 2029, alongside expanded cooperation in artificial intelligence (AI) and other emerging industries. Wi said Lee would also propose closer cooperation in cultural industries to support South Korean businesses operating in the region.

"According to the White House, U.S. President Donald Trump will prioritize reducing nuclear weapons and preventing their proliferation. NEWSLETTER · 15 Nov 2026, 20:34

😺 Oakland workers bargained for AI protections

PLUS: ChatGPT Wallet, a $300M LangChain round, and LEGO builds from a photo. ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌ ‌

November 15, 2026 | Listen online | Read online

Welcome, humans.

So Princeton researchers tried teaching an AI to learn its first words the way a baby does. They played it recordings of everyday speech from a baby’s home in the order she heard it. By its second simulated birthday, the AI still couldn’t recognize a single word.

Then they borrowed an idea from sleep. At the end of each simulated day, the AI replayed a little of what it had heard over the past few days. That worked. By the same age, it could recognize thousands of words. The same approach worked with recordings from seven other homes. But, does the AI talk like a toddler??

The experiment gives researchers a way to test how replay might help babies take that first step, and how AI can keep learning without forgetting what it heard before.

Next benchmark: toddler selective hearing. They can hear “ice cream” from upstairs. They can’t hear “clean up” from right next to you.

Here’s what happened in AI today:

😺 Oakland workers reached a tentative deal on AI job protections.

📰 ChatGPT Wallet lets your agent pay after you approve.

📰 LangChain reportedly raised $300M at a $3B valuation.

🍪 Mister Blocks suggests builds from a photo of your LEGO bricks.

🎓 Compare vendor quotes in the new Claude Sheets.

Advertise to 700K readers of The Neuron here!

😸 Oakland city workers vote on AI job protections

Oakland city workers are about to vote on a contract that spells out what happens if AI takes over their jobs. Under the proposed deal, the city would have to retrain or reassign workers displaced by AI.

Say Oakland brings in a tool that cuts the time it takes to process permit applications. The city could use those hours to clear a backlog, but it could also decide it needs fewer people processing permits. The tool saves time either way, but what happens to the people doing the work is a choice the city makes.

For Julian Ware, who works in the city’s IT department, losing jobs was only one concern. He said the union also worried workers would be held responsible for what AI produced, especially if it left them with more work. For once, I’d like the last slide of an AI demo to say “Here’s what happens to your paycheck.”

The city and IFPTE Local 21 reached a tentative three-year deal on October 31, and the union called off its planned November 5 strike. The deal includes a 9% raise over three years.

Other public workers have secured a say in what happens before the software shows up, too.

At Berkeley Unified, the same union secured 60 days’ notice before the district introduces AI, a chance to discuss the rollout, and training if the district goes ahead. The contract also bars using AI to evaluate or discipline those workers.

In Columbus, Ohio, the transit agency needs the union’s consent to put self-driving vehicles into service. The contract also protects bus drivers and mechanics from layoffs or pay cuts caused by new technology.

In New York, a state law protects covered public workers from losing their jobs or having their regular hours, pay, or benefits cut because their employer uses AI.

And what to do with the hours AI saves? After a yearlong ChatGPT pilot, Pennsylvania said it would look for ways to put that time back into public services. The state also brought union members and managers together to talk through where AI should be used.

I’d watch what Oakland offers the first worker whose job changes. Is there a role they can move into at the same pay? And do they get time on the clock to learn it?

You can see the same fight outside city hall. At Microsoft-owned Blizzard, workers won the right to bargain over how the company uses AI on the job. In Hollywood, producers now have to bargain with the actors’ union before using AI-generated performers.

As these tools take on more of the work, expect more unions to ask what happens to the people doing it now, while there’s still time to put an answer in the contract. An interesting contrast at the same time, specifically with government workers. The governments also have a responsibility to taxpayers to be financially responsible, and they aren’t represented in the votes, so there could eventually be pushback from directions..

🎓 AI Skill of the Day: Compare two quotes in Claude Sheets

Anthropic just launched Claude Sheets in beta on paid plans. Ask for a spreadsheet in chat and Claude builds one you can edit right there, with formulas you can click into and check.

Say you’re comparing software plans, printing quotes, or contractor estimates. One option has a lower base fee. The other charges less per seat, copy, or hour. Put both in a sheet so you can change the quantity once and see which deal is cheaper.

Paste this into a Claude chat:

Build a Claude Sheet to compare these two quotes: [paste quotes here] Use one editable cell for the quantity I expect to buy: [number and unit, like 40 seats]. Give each quote its own row with an editable base fee and price per unit. Use formulas to multiply the quantity by each unit price, add the base fee, and highlight the lower total. If costs recur, compare them over the same period. Flag any fees or minimums that still need to be included.

Put your quotes side by side and change the quantity to see both totals update. If you’re deciding how many software seats to buy or how large an order to place, you can see whether the same quote stays cheaper as your needs change.

Tech Policy Press

1 item in this evidence packet.

NEWSLETTER · 15 Nov 2026, 16:55

Jensen Huang is out. The scandal is just beginning.

View in browser

Top stories on Tech Policy Press

Good morning!

The biggest news in tech over the past week is the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and the various scandals that surround it. Even as details continue to emerge over Huang’s affairs and business deals, questions are swirling around the Trump administration’s handling of the situation, and over whether Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick acted to shield Nvidia. Despite his misdeeds, Huang still has his supporters in Silicon Valley—including Elon Musk and other men who don’t think his mistakes should have resulted in his resignation. President Donald Trump, perhaps unsurprisingly, had urged Huang to fight for his job.

One question is how the controversy will affect US-China relations, specifically US policy toward China on chips and AI more generally. On Tech Policy Press, Georgetown’s Mark MacCarthy wrote about what Jensen Huang’s resignation means for next week’s US-China talks in Shenzen: tighter US export controls appear likely, but the AI safety dialogue still seems on track. “Huang’s downfall will also reignite the debate over banning Chinese open-weight models. Nvidia was one of the leading voices defending open-weight models, even when there was reportedly a push in Washington to ban them,” he writes.

While Huang's resignation dominated global headlines, EU leaders gathered in Dublin on Friday for the bloc's first dedicated discussion on AI, seeking to strengthen Europe's AI sovereignty and reduce its dependence on foreign technology providers. Yet Ireland's approach raises questions about these ambitions. Layla Wade writes that Ireland, which holds the Council presidency, is drafting revisions to the Cloud and AI Development Act that could favor the Big Tech firms Europe wants to depend on less.

Beyond questions of infrastructure and industrial policy, Europe's reliance on foreign technology providers also raises security concerns. Tech Policy Press fellow Liz Carolan explored how the Dublin summits brought these challenges into focus, from the disruption of Ukraine's access to Starlink to Europe's limited alternatives, highlighting the tension between competitiveness and safety in Europe's approach to tech policy.

The European Commission also unveiled its long-awaited Digital Fairness Act (DFA) proposal this week, renewing debate about how to protect consumers from manipulative online practices.

Four consumer law scholars, Anne-Lise Sibony, Fabrizio Esposito, Cristiana Santos and Cecilia Isola, assess what the proposal gets right and where its scope, coherence and capacity to protect consumers remain in question. Their analysis includes an original Tech Policy Press graphic mapping what the DFA would ban outright and what it would require companies to do.

Itxaso Domínguez de Olazábal of EDRi writes that the proposal recognizes addictive design but relies on opt-outs, narrows protection for vulnerable consumers and does not clearly require companies to prove their practices are fair.

‘A New Agenda for Tech Regulation’

Multiple authors this week wrote about the opportunity to reform technology, particularly following the US midterm elections.

Alice Marwick, director of research at Data & Society, asked leading scholars and policy experts what’s on their wishlist for tech regulation at the state and federal level — beyond AI. The answers span affordability, privacy, ad transparency, scams, data access and regulatory capacity. “Expanding regulatory capacity, passing comprehensive privacy legislation, and mandating data access for researchers may not be flashy, but these policies will have real-world implications for technology companies and, potentially, enormous benefits for the average American,” she writes.

Greg LeRoy, who founded Good Jobs First, counts 44 of 45 local data center ballot measures passing by an average of 77% — and writes that incoming governors and state legislators have “a terrific opportunity in 2027 to enact effective reforms.”

“Companies cannot both be a conduit when sued and a speaker when regulated,” write Allison Stanger, a distinguished endowed professor at Middlebury College, and Bruce Schneier, a fellow at the Berkman Klein Center for Internet & Society at Harvard University, who propose three steps Congress could take on liability, amplification and disclosure.

Richard Reisman, non-resident senior fellow at the Foundation for American Innovation, writes that “the platforms we call ‘social media’ are becoming increasingly less social” — even as AI takes over their role in filtering information. His proposal: treat both social media and AI as one project, and judge every piece of the whole by whose interests it serves.

Will the AI-powered internet “remain open to anyone with a good idea,” or will visibility online “depend on the preferences of a few dominant platforms”? Jared Sine, chief strategy & legal officer at GoDaddy, on why open standards matter now.

The US House Judiciary Committee under Rep. Jim Jordan has spent four years investigating disinformation researchers and Europe’s Digital Services Act. Tech Policy Press deputy editor Ramsha Jahangir and contributing editor Dean Jackson ask what changes now that Jordan is leaving the chairmanship.

Big settlements, little accountability for Meta and TikTok

States will receive at least $1.2 billion a year from Meta for ten years, and up to $16.7 billion in total. Erie Meyer and Stephanie T. Nguyen, senior fellows at the Center for Law and the Economy, say the money should buy the capacity to check whether the company is actually complying.

Recent Meta and TikTok settlements may “define the invisible, state-backed rules shaping information access for a generation of young people,” Daphne Keller, director of platform regulation at Stanford Law School's Program in Law, Science & Technology, writes — struck in backroom deals with “no opportunity for meaningful public oversight or challenge by affected internet users.” (This is a substantial piece of work; I urge anyone thinking about the settlements to take a look.)

On Nov. 12, the case brought by Abrham Meareg, Fisseha Tekle and the Katiba Institute against Meta returned to Kenya’s High Court. Wairimu Gathimba, a writer and researcher in Kenya’s social justice movement, on what’s at stake over hate speech in Ethiopia.

The United Nations and AI governance

Machines are already killing on their own. Next week, leaders gather at the UN in Geneva to decide whether to negotiate a treaty on autonomous weapons. León Castellanos-Jankiewicz of the University of Amsterdam: “If diplomats fail to negotiate a new treaty, ever more lives will hang by a line of code.”

António Guterres is stepping down after a decade as UN Secretary-General. “His departure comes at a critical moment for global AI governance,” write the Center for AI and Digital Policy’s Merve Hickok, Marc Rotenberg, Ren Bin Lee Dixon, Idil I. Kaner and Tatjana Titareva. The selection of the next Secretary-General is crucial.

Down the rabbit hole: the need for improved recommender-system controls

The European Commission preliminarily found last week that TikTok failed to adequately assess and mitigate the “rabbit hole effect” of its For You feed. Cecilia Isola, research fellow at SERICS, writes about what the Commission’s findings mean for how recommender systems are tested under the DSA. “Ensuring that systemic risk assessments do not become exercises in documenting compliance requires scrutiny not only of the procedures followed, but also of the evidentiary basis and methodological reasoning supporting the conclusions reached,” she writes.

Christoph Trattner, professor of information science at the University of Bergen, argues recommender-system controls should honor what users say they want now, and proposes three requirements: instructions that last as long as promised, targeted ways to suspend an inferred interest, and explanations of what fills the feed afterward.

The OpenAI copyright fight

56 amicus briefs were filed in the OpenAI copyright litigation. A closer look at the briefs reveals the arguments at play in the fight over AI and copyright, who is making them, and what is at stake. Check out the graphic on the precedents referenced and let me know what you think.

Anthropic kicks off IPO push

Keith Porcaro, Assistant Clinical Professor of Law at Duke Law School, examines Anthropic’s dual-class structure: two shares with no economic rights control the company. He asks what the government would have to pay to seize them. “This vulnerability is not unique to Anthropic. Other AI companies with imbalanced governance structures may be seizable for less than their full economic value. This is the cost of concentrating control in a small number of people, while working on a capable, potentially dangerous technology,” he writes.

On funding Black women public interest tech leaders

Marking the victories of numerous Black women elected leaders, Fallon S. Wilson of #BlackTechFutures Research Institute writes a personal missive calling for “delusional funding” of Black women public interest tech leaders.

More important news from around the world

Cambodia’s government says no online scam compounds remain in the country. Tech Policy Press fellow Lam Le reports on how scam operators are relocating across borders, recruiting experienced workers through Telegram and rebuilding smaller operations. “The lack of protection for survivors of trafficking into scam operations has contributed to a humanitarian crisis, pushing some to return to scamming simply to earn enough money to survive. Meanwhile, the spread of smaller scam operations around the world makes them harder for authorities to detect and dismantle,” she reports.

Yasodara Cordova, an independent consultant and part-time researcher at CTS-FGV, argues Brazil’s AI strategy is tilted toward supercomputers and centralized data, and that edge AI — inference on local hardware — deserves a much louder debate. “Brazil already has centrally standardized Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) that allows dozens of private and public companies to plug into shared protocols, such as the Pix payment system. However, another big potential for the country resides at the margins, in the opportunity to automate what already exists or create new manufacturing lines supported by edge AI,” she writes.

Must reads ideas upstream of the AI policy debate

Matthew Kirschenbaum, Commonwealth Professor of Artificial Intelligence and English at the University of Virginia, traces a line from 1970s phone phreaking to today’s agentic AI: in both, the vulnerability is the porous boundary between language and code.

Eryk Salvaggio, a Gates Scholar researching AI and the humanities at the University of Cambridge, reads the “Pain Axis” paper behind the “AI torture chamber” fight. What it showed was “narrower”: a vector that encourages models to associate descriptions of pain with moral failure and worthlessness.

Politics, government and social media influencers

New York City can set the standard for how governments work with influencers, or it can keep improvising. Columbia’s Anya Schiffrin, author of a recent report on the subject, spoke to me about which way the city should go on the podcast.

What we’re watching

On Friday, Nov. 6, I moderated a discussion between Meg Young, PhD, Khari Johnson, Sareeta Amrute and Hannah Sassaman on how AI civics can help us meet the moment and realize a more democratic vision of public interest technology. If you weren't able to join for the event, the resources and recording are now available on Data & Society’s website.

Are you a university student, PhD candidate, or recent graduate with an interest in competition law, economics or policy? Are you concerned about the dangers of concentrated economic power? Do you have views on how it could, or should, be countered? If so, consider entering the inaugural New Competition School Award for Best Commentary by November 30. The winning entry and up to three runners-up will be edited and published in Tech Policy Press.

I wish you the best for the week ahead!

-Justin

NEWSLETTER · 15 Nov 2026, 14:32

On GPS: GOP’s Future, Democrats’ Accountability Push, China’s Spy Goals

Today’s guests and topics, plus: Huang’s fall and the AI boom, AI is not a chokepoint, and what foreign policy did Americans vote for? …

ADVERTISEMENT

Sunday, November 15, 2026 View in browser

Insights, analysis and must reads from CNN’s Fareed Zakaria and the Global Public Square team, compiled by Global Briefing editor Chris Good

Seeing this newsletter as a forward? Sign up here.

On Today’s Show

On GPS, at 10 a.m. and 1 p.m. ET:

How, amid America’s alienation from the major political parties, did the Democrats pull off a midterm landslide? Fareed argues they did so by becoming an anti-party party.

Rather than choose between moving to the left or center, they did both. Power flowed from the grassroots, recalling an earlier era in American history when political parties were rooted in their regions and were less ideologically conformist. Fareed says this might show the way toward restoring the political party itself and repairing politics.

Where does the Republican Party go from here? President Donald Trump’s hostile takeover sidelined the old, Reaganite GOP orthodoxy of free markets and free trade. Will the party reassume its pre-Trump form, abandoning MAGA? Fareed talks with bestselling author David Brooks.

Government corruption was a major concern for Americans in the midterm elections, with 90% saying it was “crucial” or a “major factor” in determining their vote. But could pursuing a slew of investigations into the Trump administration backfire on the Democrats? Emily Bazelon, staff writer at The New York Times Magazine, joins Fareed to discuss.

This week, the world witnessed Jensen Huang’s dramatic exit from chip designer Nvidia, after reports that US officials believed he was having an affair with a potential Chinese intelligence asset. What does the episode tell us about Chinese espionage? Fareed is joined by Julian Gewirtz, who was senior director for China and Taiwan on President Joe Biden’s National Security Council.

Finally: a preview of Fareed’s latest CNN special. Tonight at 8 p.m. ET and PT, Fareed will host “Iran and the Bomb,” an hourlong documentary examining the complicated history of Iran’s nuclear program—which began in the 1950s with US support and, for the last four decades, has been a major point of contention for America. It’s important context that helps explain why the US is at war today.

ADVERTISEMENT

Jensen Huang Fell. Will AI, Too?

Around the world, AI-related stocks were trading lower after the precipitous fall of Nvidia’s Jensen Huang, as Reuters’ Sashwat Chauhan reported this week. Huang has been an important impresario, not just of Nvidia but of the AI boom as a whole, Tim Higgins wrote midweek for The Wall Street Journal, warning that Huang’s fall will test the staying power of AI’s attractiveness to investors.

Huang’s exit aside, The Economist’s current cover story suggests we should expect an AI-driven financial crash for other reasons, too. “After the midterms the NASDAQ index of tech firms fell by 5.5% in a day, as investors fretted that Democrats would block data centres,” the magazine writes. “When Anthropic floats [i.e. makes its IPO], bears argue, its prospects will be visible to all in its stock price, a fall in which could trigger a market-wide rout. Anthropic and its main rival, OpenAI, are the customer-facing companies feeding the entire AI supply chain, including data centres, chip designers and chipmakers. If they have too few sales or cannot charge high enough prices, disaster looms.”

Nobody Dominates AI Completely

In recent years, geopolitical analysts have noted the rising importance of “chokepoints,” which countries can weaponize to their advantage. For example: Iran gained massive leverage by harassing shipping through the Strait of Hormuz, choking off global oil flows. The US exerts control over its own chokepoint, its globally important banking system and the centrality of the US dollar to world finance, by levying financial sanctions on its adversaries and locking them out.

AI might not work like that, Max Bessler writes for the magazine Palladium. As US AI labs pursue “closed” models, which for now are more advanced, while Chinese developers offer “open-weighted” models that are customizable and cheaper, Bessler foresees a split global market for AI services. With AI on offer from two different sources, neither will be able to coerce others by threatening to close off access to AI completely. That said, open and closed AI models seem to have different strengths and weaknesses, and their use cases will be different, too. “Buyers will pay a premium when the gap in capabilities is worth it, but below that, they decide based on cost,” Bessler writes. “Motivated businesses and savvy consumers are already waking up to the advantages in splitting their use between open and closed AI models, and open models are flooding the market from Chinese firms.”

Washington can maximize its AI-driven geopolitical leverage by playing nice with global AI customers and keeping US-made AI models as popular as possible around the world, Bessler argues. “To ensure that the United States holds this chokepoint,” Bessler writes, “Washington should keep chip controls narrow and keep the clouds American,” with cloud computing performed by data centers in the US. “Maybe the U.S. can no longer tell you what model to use, but it can own where that model runs.”

What Foreign Policy Did America Vote For?

Democrats ran—and won big—on opposing the Iran war, which has raised fuel prices and the already-high cost of living. But as many commentators have noted, Democrats’ foreign-policy views are not monolithic. Israel policy, especially, stands out as a source of division.

In The New York Review of Books, which has published midterm reactions from various journalists and commentators, Aslı Ü. Bâli and Aziz Rana reflect on what a new, post-Trump, midterm-responsive Democratic foreign policy might look like: “At a minimum, this new posture would involve rejecting elective and preventive wars, refusing to conduct policy by sanction and blockade, and investing in what actually produces security: climate, health, and constraints on oligarchic power, alongside multilateral engagement and legal compliance. Getting there will require, among other things, identifying and empowering a new generation of foreign policy thinkers to staff the next administration. … This will be difficult, especially in a divided Democratic caucus whose leaders have little interest in asking where unconstrained US power has led the country and the globe. But Americans have voiced their discontent in terms unparalleled since Watergate.”

Xu Gao: China's Problem Is Too Little Debt, Not Too Much

The Bank of China International's Chief Economist argues that excess savings and weak domestic demand make investment-led growth sustainable, even as returns decline.

͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­͏ ­

Forwarded this email? Subscribe here for more

Xu Gao: China's Problem Is Too Little Debt, Not Too Much

The Bank of China International's Chief Economist argues that excess savings and weak domestic demand make investment-led growth sustainable, even as returns decline.

Yuxuan JIA and Yixin Guo

Nov 15

READ IN APP

Xu Gao is the Chief Economist and Assistant President of Bank of China International Co. Ltd., an investment bank, and an adjunct professor at the National School of Development (NSD) at Peking University.

He delivered a TED-style talk on China’s healthcare challenges and proposed reforms at TAIXUE, a programme of the New Economist think tank, on 29 January 2026. A transcript was published on the New Economist’s official WeChat blog on 10 September, and the video recording is available on TAIXUE’s YouTube channel.

Xu has reviewed the following translation before publication.

徐高：宏观思维如何理解宏观经济

Xu Gao: Understanding the Macroeconomy Through Macroeconomic Thinking

Hello, everyone. I am Xu Gao. I am delighted to talk to you today about the macroeconomy — a familiar subject that many people do not really understand. I would like to make five points.

My first point is that the macroeconomy is different from the microeconomy. Understanding the macroeconomy requires macroeconomic thinking.

Everyone is familiar with the idea of the macroeconomy. Everyone is part of it and has their own understanding of it. That can make it seem as though anyone can talk about macroeconomics. Yet for many people, the macroeconomy is actually unfamiliar and even rather strange, because some of the mechanisms and principles at work are rarely encountered in everyday life.

Consider a microeconomic entity such as a company. Every company operates within an external environment that it cannot control and must take as given. Suppose that, because of weak economic conditions, a company suddenly finds that market demand for its products has fallen. It struggles to sell its products, and its revenue declines. How should it respond to this shock? The intuitive answer is to cut costs and improve efficiency: reduce spending to protect profit margins and weather the downturn in demand.

No business owner would say, “Demand is weak, and my revenue has fallen, so I should pay my employees more.” Because even if higher wages gave the employees greater purchasing power and they spent some of that money on the company’s products, the company would still be very small relative to the market in which it operates.

In other words, even if the employees are among the company’s customers, they would make up only a tiny share of its customer base. Raising their income will not reverse the decline in market demand for the company’s products. It will simply increase costs, reduce the profit margin, and make it harder for the company to weather the downturn.

For a microeconomic entity facing a shock from weak external demand, the appropriate response is, of course, to cut costs and improve efficiency. Applying the same logic to the macroeconomy, however, would be a mistake.

Consider macroeconomic policy. When demand is weak across the economy, businesses in many industries struggle to sell their products, and government revenue is consequently low; how should the government respond? Cutting costs and reducing expenditure are not the right answer, because government policy itself has a substantial influence on the macroeconomic environment in which the government operates.

Government expenditure is an important source of private-sector income. If the government cuts spending when economy-wide demand is already insufficient, private-sector income will fall further. Households and businesses will become less willing to spend, aggravating the demand shortfall. Government tax revenue will then decline further as well.

In macroeconomics, the appropriate policy response to insufficient demand is therefore not contraction but expansion. By increasing expenditure, the government can raise private-sector income, support economic activity, and ease the shortfall in demand across the economy. The logic of the macroeconomy is thus the opposite of that of the microeconomy.

Why is that? When analysing macroeconomic policy, it is essential to recognise that government policy changes the macroeconomic environment, which in turn affects the government’s own revenue. A feedback loop can therefore develop between expenditure and revenue: in some circumstances, the more the government spends, the more revenue it ultimately receives; the more it cuts spending, the more its revenue declines. This is difficult to grasp from the perspective of everyday experience. It seems counterintuitive and contrary to common sense.

The macroeconomy is therefore very different from the microeconomy. Everyday experience alone is not enough to understand it. Even so-called common sense can be misleading when applied to macroeconomics. This does not mean that common sense is invariably wrong, but it may sometimes lead to the wrong conclusion.

A genuine understanding of the macroeconomy requires macroeconomic thinking. What does that mean? It means considering the different actors in the economy, the complex causal relationships between them, and the feedback effects they generate, so that the macroeconomy can be understood as a whole.

Without macroeconomic thinking, a small error in economic reasoning can lead to a seriously mistaken conclusion. If macroeconomic policies are designed or implemented without this perspective, they can produce a fallacy of composition: each policy may appear reasonable on its own, yet their combined effect may be entirely different from the desired outcome.

My second point is that the root of insufficient demand lies in income distribution.

Insufficient demand has been a major problem for the Chinese economy for more than two decades. In 2000, the Chinese government was already identifying insufficient effective demand as a problem. By 2025, insufficient demand was still being cited as a prominent challenge. It has therefore been a persistent problem for the Chinese economy.

But where does insufficient demand come from? On closer examination, the idea appears paradoxical. Demand is commonly associated with human wants, which are unlimited, whereas supply — however much it expands — must ultimately remain finite. If unlimited wants confront finite supply, how can demand possibly be insufficient?

This is where a deeper macroeconomic understanding becomes necessary. Demand does not refer simply to what people want. It means the willingness of people or other economic actors to spend, backed by the income needed to make purchases in the market. Economists call this “effective demand.”

The longstanding problem facing the Chinese economy is therefore insufficient effective demand. If human wants are unlimited, the problem would appear to be one of purchasing power. Yet money is a constraint for every individual economic actor.

At the macroeconomic level, however, the quantity of money is not a constraint, because a sovereign government can issue currency. What matters behind a country’s money is its purchasing power, which ultimately comes from what the country produces. A country’s total output is also its total income. Viewed as a macroeconomic whole, China therefore generates enough purchasing power to buy everything it produces. In a monetary economy, this purchasing power takes the form of money, and there is enough of it in aggregate.

That income, however, must be distributed among different economic actors and matched with their willingness to spend before it can become effective demand. If income distribution produces a mismatch — leaving those willing to spend without sufficient purchasing power, while those with purchasing power lack the willingness to spend — aggregate purchasing power cannot readily be converted into effective demand. Money accumulates among those who are unwilling to spend it, becoming excess savings rather than actual purchases. This is how a shortage of aggregate, or effective, demand emerges.

The next question is who might have the money to spend but be unwilling to do so. Or, to put it more bluntly, who has money but doesn’t want to spend it? Consumers do not fit this description because human wants, including the desire to consume, are unlimited. When consumers earn more, they will spend more. Investors may behave differently. Their willingness to invest depends heavily on the expected rate of return. If the return falls to a very low level — if 100 yuan invested this year is expected to become only 90 yuan next year — even someone with plenty of money will be unwilling to invest.

The willingness to invest is therefore not unlimited; it is affected by the rate of return. If too little of an economy’s total income flows to households and consumers, while too much flows to investors, effective demand can easily become insufficient. If consumers receive only a small share of total income, consumption will inevitably be insufficient.

Under such conditions, demand tends to depend more on investment. Yet investment is subject to diminishing marginal returns: as its scale increases, its rate of return declines. As returns fall, investors will eventually become less willing to invest, weakening investment-driven demand. If income is not redirected to consumers in time, investors will hold on to money rather than spend it, resulting in insufficient effective demand, or insufficient domestic demand.

Therefore, the principal reason China has experienced insufficient effective or domestic demand for more than two decades is that the household sector receives too small a share of total income.

China’s economy has long tended to favour investment over consumption. When China was still an economy of scarcity, that tendency played an important positive role: it produced a high investment rate, rapid capital accumulation, and correspondingly rapid economic growth. But conditions have changed over the past two decades. China has moved from an economy of scarcity to one of excess supply. Insufficient demand and consumption have become major constraints on growth. Continuing to prioritise investment over consumption under these circumstances will aggravate both problems.

To tackle weak consumption and domestic demand at their source, income distribution must be improved. Suppose, for example, that state-owned assets transferred to social security funds could be credited to individuals’ social security accounts, so that people could receive annual dividends and withdraw the money to spend immediately. This would provide more direct support for consumer income and consumption and provide a powerful boost to the transition towards consumption-led growth. The fundamental remedy for insufficient demand is therefore to improve the distribution of income and raise the household sector’s share of economy-wide income.

In the long run, there are only four possible scenarios for addressing insufficient domestic demand. Three of them are within China’s control: the optimal, suboptimal, and non-optimal strategies.

In an economy facing insufficient demand, the fundamental problem is that income distribution holds back consumption, leaving domestic demand insufficient. One possible response is to rely on external demand. External demand, however, is beyond China’s control and must largely be taken as given. When it is strong, it can generate an export-led boom. From 2003 until the global financial crisis, for example, strong external demand helped China record consecutive years of double-digit growth, at times even producing economic overheating.

China’s economy is now much larger than it was in 2008 and is also very large relative to other economies. At the same time, global trade protectionism has clearly been on the rise. If external demand comes under increasing pressure, China will need to find domestic solutions to the constraints that insufficient demand places on economic growth.

There are three possible domestic strategies, and the logic is straightforward. To rebalance supply and demand when domestic demand is insufficient, an economy must either add to demand or reduce supply. Domestic demand consists essentially of consumption and investment, so adding to demand means either increasing consumption or stimulating investment. Alongside reducing supply, these amount to the three possible domestic strategies.

The optimal strategy is the approach described earlier: improve income distribution and direct a larger share of income to households, thereby strengthening consumption and addressing insufficient domestic demand at its source. A successful transition towards consumption-led growth would also allow ordinary people to share more fully in the benefits of economic growth.

The purpose of China’s development is to meet the people’s growing needs for a better life, and those needs are reflected to a significant extent in higher consumption. Only by improving income distribution, transitioning towards consumption-led growth, and boosting household spending can China better fulfil the ultimate purpose of economic development. That is why this is the optimal strategy.

But the difficulty is that pursuing this strategy requires changes to income distribution. Such changes are extremely difficult, and the obstacles are not even primarily economic. Although there is now a strong consensus on the need to boost consumption and raise household incomes, it will take time to achieve substantive progress on the necessary policies.

The suboptimal strategy is investment. If it is difficult to add substantially to consumption, investment is the remaining way to increase demand. Ultimately, the choice is whether to continue investing. Continuing to create demand and stabilise growth through investment is often described as returning to the old investment-led model. This is the suboptimal strategy, intended to maintain a stable balance between supply and demand and thereby keep economic growth steady.

However, the suboptimal strategy has a consequence: as investment expands, its rate of return will continue to fall, potentially turning many projects into low-efficiency or ineffective investments. But if these concerns actually bring investment to a halt, consumption, investment, and external demand could all remain weak. With no way to boost demand, the economy would then be forced to adjust through a contraction in supply.

China experienced such an adjustment between 1998 and 2002, five consecutive years of deflation. In 1998, China set a goal of helping struggling state-owned enterprises overcome their difficulties within three years. That was the non-optimal strategy: when demand could not be kept sufficiently strong, the supply side underwent large-scale capacity reduction. Such an adjustment is likely to be the most damaging of the three strategies.

These are the four possible long-term scenarios for addressing insufficient domestic demand and the three strategies available to China. Logically, these four paths cover all the possibilities. External demand is beyond China’s control, and a consumption-led transition will take time. For now, that leaves a choice between the suboptimal strategy of investment-led growth and the non-optimal strategy of cutting capacity.

This brings me to my fourth point: the suboptimal, investment-led strategy is entirely sustainable, but its sustainability can be understood only through macroeconomic thinking.

First, many people will argue that investment-led growth requires investment to keep expanding, eventually weakening the incentive to invest and leading to more inefficient or ineffective investment projects. Are such projects not a waste of resources? That is a valid question, and the answer is yes: inefficient or ineffective investment does waste resources. The next question, however, is whether resources would cease to be wasted if those investments were not made. If abandoning them destabilised demand, the resulting waste would be even greater.

Where does the waste begin? With the distribution of income. When insufficient income is directed to consumers, economic development has already deviated from its ultimate purpose of meeting the people’s needs for a better life. To achieve all these objectives, the best course would be to pursue a transition towards consumption-led growth — the optimal strategy. But if that option is not yet feasible, the suboptimal strategy remains considerably better than the non-optimal one.

Second, many argue that under an investment-led growth model, declining returns will become increasingly unlikely to cover financing costs. Debt financing already dominates China’s financial system, so any expansion in investment will necessarily be accompanied by more debt. China’s debt level is already high. According to the Bank for International Settlements’ measure of total credit to the non-financial sector, China’s debt-to-GDP ratio was close to 300 per cent in the second quarter of 2025. This was above the average for advanced economies, let alone that for developing economies. In 2008, the ratio was below 150 per cent. It has therefore almost doubled.

Whether viewed across countries or over time, the apparent conclusions are that China has too much domestic debt and that its debt is growing rapidly. This naturally raises the question of whether such a large and fast-growing debt burden will trigger a crisis. A widely held view is that China should therefore reduce debt — or at least slow its growth — through deleveraging policies to contain the risk. This line of reasoning is very popular.

However, simply observing that China has a higher debt ratio than other countries does not establish that its debt is excessive or that a crisis is imminent. The methodology is fundamentally flawed. Whether there is too much or too little of something depends not primarily on its absolute quantity, but on the balance between supply and demand. China’s debt must be examined within that supply-and-demand balance before its scale can be judged excessive or insufficient.

China does have a large volume of debt, but it is also an economy with excess savings. Its savings rate has fallen significantly since 2008, when it exceeded 50 per cent. It has declined by roughly 10 percentage points but remains above 40 per cent — around twice the average level in many other countries. China is therefore an excess-savings economy.

Savings constitute the supply of debt, while debt represents demand for those savings. The scale of China’s debt must be assessed in relation to the balance between savings and borrowing, not by examining debt alone.

There is little doubt that China currently has excess savings. Large pools of savings are looking for investment opportunities or other outlets but struggle to find suitable ones. This is clearly visible in the financial markets. One prominent phenomenon in China’s financial system in recent years has been a “shortage of investable assets”: financial institutions hold substantial funds but cannot find suitable projects in which to invest. Behind this financial phenomenon lies large amounts of savings that have nowhere to go. It is the manifestation of an excess of savings over demand for borrowing.

When savings are excessive, even a large volume of debt may still be insufficient. Conversely, if a country has excess domestic demand and insufficient savings, even a small amount of debt may be excessive. Looking at the absolute size of China’s debt alone tells very little. Debt must be considered within the supply-and-demand relationship between borrowing and savings. Given that China has insufficient domestic demand and excess savings, the present problem is not too much debt, but too little.

Some will surely ask: where do these excess savings come from? As explained earlier, insufficient domestic demand arises when income distribution creates a mismatch between purchasing power and the willingness to spend. Some economic actors have purchasing power but little desire to spend it, so their purchasing power is not fully converted into effective demand. This unspent purchasing power accumulates in their hands and becomes the source of excess savings. An economy with insufficient domestic demand, such as China, must therefore also have excess savings. For an economy with excess savings, the size of its debt is not the problem.

These two considerations show that the suboptimal, investment-led strategy is entirely sustainable in China. A declining rate of return need not make the model impossible to maintain. In fact, in his phenomenal The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money, John Maynard Keynes even put forward a seemingly extraordinary idea: when demand is insufficient, the government could pay people to dig holes, fill them in, and then dig them again. This is commonly known as Keynes’s “digging holes” argument.

But think about it: what is the point of digging holes? What return could such an activity generate, especially if the holes are simply filled in again? The value of digging holes only becomes clear when viewed from a macroeconomic perspective. Although the activity itself appears to create no value, it can be seen as a way of transferring income to unemployed workers — a form of work relief. If money cannot be given directly to the public, paying people to do such work offers an indirect alternative. Its greater value lies in its wider social benefits: stabilising employment, supporting demand, and sustaining economic growth.

Therefore, when assessing the return on investment under this suboptimal strategy, it is not enough to focus solely on the cash flow generated by individual projects. The wider social benefits created by investment must also be considered. Taking those social benefits into account, alongside the broader conditions of insufficient domestic demand and excess savings, shows why this suboptimal strategy is entirely sustainable.

My fifth and final point is that, today, the world is ruled by mere ideas.

In the final paragraph of the last chapter of his great book The General Theory, Keynes made the following famous observation: “…the ideas of economists and political philosophers, both when they are right and when they are wrong, are more powerful than is commonly understood. Indeed the world is ruled by little else. Practical men, who believe themselves to be quite exempt from any intellectual influences, are usually the slaves of some defunct economist.”

The macro and micro levels differ considerably. Many constraints that appear fixed at the micro level are not fixed at the macro level; they are endogenous.

For a company or an individual, money is certainly a constraint. At the macro level, however, the money supply is not fixed, because the government can print it. Demand is also a constraint for an individual company: if market demand for its products is weak, it cannot sell them. But at the macro level, government policies can regulate and create demand, so demand is not a fixed constraint on the macroeconomy. Supply may seem an obvious constraint. Once a company has reached the limits of its production capacity, it cannot simply produce more. At the macro level, however, structural policies can expand the economy’s productive capacity. At least in the long run, supply capacity is not a fixed constraint either.

What, then, is the true constraint on the macroeconomy?

The discussion so far has shown that even if external circulation is weak and external demand comes under severe pressure, China still has three domestic strategies: the optimal, the suboptimal, and the non-optimal. Why, then, has downward pressure on economic growth persisted in recent years? The reason is simple: there is still insufficient understanding of how the macroeconomy actually works. Without recognising the importance of income distribution to stable, long-term economic growth, or the need to improve it to support a transition towards consumption-led growth, the optimal strategy will remain out of reach. Likewise, without recognising that the supposedly “old path” of investment-led growth can be sustained through macroeconomic thinking, policymakers will not dare to keep stimulating investment.

The real constraint, then, is a mistaken understanding of the economy arising from a lack of macroeconomic thinking. In my view, this misreading has much to do with the economics education in China.

The current mainstream Western macroeconomic framework is known academically as the “New Neoclassical Synthesis.” Most people have probably never heard the term. Yet this is precisely the kind of idea Keynes said rules the world. People may never have heard its name, but it still shapes their lives and the world they live in.

In essence, the New Neoclassical Synthesis is a form of microeconomics. The role of effective demand, and the feedback effects between spending and income discussed earlier, are largely absent from this mainstream Western macroeconomic framework. Why is that? This framework matured in the United States, whose circumstances are the opposite of China’s. China faces insufficient demand, whereas the United States faces excess demand and insufficient supply. So a framework developed in the context of the US economy largely reflects the workings of an economy constrained by supply. The logic of a supply-constrained macroeconomy is, to a significant extent, similar to that governing an individual company. In this sense, mainstream Western macroeconomics is essentially microeconomic.

The feedback effects discussed earlier are what make macroeconomics so rich and interesting, yet they are difficult to find in mainstream Western macroeconomics. As a result, many people trained in Western economics see both the world and China through the lens of this mainstream paradigm. This makes it difficult for them to understand China’s economy as it actually operates.

For China’s economy to overcome its current pressures, it is necessary both to learn from the history of economic thought and to draw lessons from real-world experience. Theory must be tested against reality and applied in practice. Only by using macroeconomic thinking to understand how the Chinese economy actually works can China achieve steady and sustainable growth over the long term.

Thank you.
