Understanding Positional Embeddings in Modern LLMs

The Methods Behind How LLMs Represent Token Positions

Sebastian Raschka, PhD

Nov 15

READ IN APP

This article explains how LLMs encode and process token position information by covering classic and contemporary methods like RoPE. It’s essentially a comprehensive journey where we will work through the mechanisms with visual examples, a little math, and a small PyTorch implementation. And, of course, we will then also look at how modern architectures adapt or omit explicit positional encodings.

Below, you can find a preview of which positional embedding choices are used in 2026 open-weight models. We will discuss these choices later after covering the underlying mechanisms.

Figure 1: A preview of the gallery’s 2026 architectures, grouped by positional encoding. Architecture figures taken from my LLM gallery.

But why spending so much time writing/reading about this rather minor building block? LLMs keep evolving, and as I was preparing a new article on the latest LLM architecture trends this year, I realized that I never covered the positional information methods used in LLMs in any article so far. So, instead of letting the other article get out of hand, I thought it might make sense to publish this as a standalone building block for the upcoming LLM architecture and trends analysis.

Also, over the years, many readers have asked me for a tutorial on relative positional embeddings (aka RoPE) to follow up on the learned absolute embeddings used in GPT. It’s been on my TODO list for a long time, so I finally revisited my old draft and put this walkthrough together, which will cover

different positional methods in LLMs;

(finally) how RoPE works;

what methods are used by recent open LLMs.

Note that this article assumes some familiarity with basic LLM architectures and works well as a follow-up to Build a Large Language Model (From Scratch). If the concepts and computation of the attention internals, particularly query, key, and value vectors, are a little fuzzy, I recommend checking my self-attention walkthrough as a refresher:

Understanding and Coding Self-Attention, Multi-Head Attention, Causal-Attention, and Cross-Attention in LLMs

January 14, 2024

Read full story

The article is also very technical and likely too detailed for some. However, I really wanted to have this foundation out there for future articles to reference. And no worries, the next one will return to a more comfortable style.

PS: I started experimenting with Substack’s new “perks” system. As a thank-you to supporters, I’ve added image exports and metadata from the architecture gallery for you to use in your own analyses.

PPS: If you are already familiar with how position embeddings work, feel free to fast-forward to Section 6, Positional choices in modern architectures.

Note that this article may be best read in a web browser than an email client. I recommend checking out the web view here, which also has a navigation bar on the left side.

1. Where position information enters an LLM

Let’s start with the overall architecture. A tokenizer encodes the input text into token IDs. Next, the input token embedding layer of an LLM maps each of these IDs to a vector of numbers. We usually refer to these entries in that vector as “features” (similar to classic ML). The transformer blocks inside the LLM then modify these vectors using attention and feedforward layers. Lastly, the model’s output layer produces logit scores (converted into probabilities) for the next token.

The following shows this path in GPT-2 (a relatively classic LLM) and MiniMax-M3 (a more recent LLM released this year; a placeholder for most other recent LLMs). As we can see, the architectures are not too different from each other, but before we get to the positional information parts, let’s focus on the orange blocks for now (the token embedding layers) and the transformer blocks above them; the other architecture differences aren’t needed for this explanation.

Figure 2: Token embedding layers in GPT-2 and MiniMax-M3, highlighted in orange. (Side note: M3’s first three out of 60 feedforward layers are dense not MoEs.)

A token embedding layer, shown in the figure above, maps each token ID to a vector. Position information is then added afterward. Concretely, and as we will discuss in more detail later, GPT-2 adds a position vector to each token embedding before the modified embedding enters the first transformer block. On the other hand, MiniMax-M3 adds the positional information in each transformer block using a method called RoPE inside attention.

We will talk about these methods in more detail later, but first, let’s start with the big picture information explaining why explicit positional information is added or needed.

1.1 Why add positional information?

Consider the following two input sentences as a simple example:

“The red fox jumps over the gray fox.”

“The gray fox jumps over the red fox.”

Changing the word order changes which fox does the jumping, as illustrated in the following figure.

Figure 3: Changing the word order changes which fox jumps.

For the examples in this article, we will treat each word as one token for simplicity. (As you know, real tokenizers can split words into smaller subword tokens.) The first sentence then has eight token positions, from 0 through 7.

Figure 4: Token IDs and positions in the toy sentence.

# Assume the following small vocabulary. # Real vocabularies typically have 50k to 200k entries vocab = { "The": 0, "red": 1, "fox": 2, "jumps": 3, "gray": 4, "over": 5, "the": 6, }

(If you are interested in how vocabularies are built, check out my tokenizer materials on GitHub.)

Note that “fox”, which appears twice in the input sentence, gets the same token ID 2 in both cases. And since they both have the same token ID, irrespective of the position in the input sentence, they would get the same embedding vector from the embedding layer.

Now, let’s consider a regular self-attention mechanism with no positional encoding (and no causal mask), where every input token can attend to every other token in the input. In this scenario, swapping any two words, like “red” and “gray”, would still result in the same embeddings and attention scores between each token pair.

GPT-style models do use a causal (autoregressive) mask, which restricts each token to itself and earlier tokens. Interestingly, this causal mask gives the computation some implicit order information. In our example, “jumps” can attend to “red” in the first sentence and “gray” in the second. So, a causal LLM can theoretically already distinguish these sentences even without explicit positional embeddings. (We will return to this idea later when discussing the “NoPE” concept.)

Nonetheless, GPT-2 and most newer LLMs include explicit positional information because it gives attention a direct signal about positions. That’s because even though the causal mask determines which tokens are accessible and provides implicit order information, it doesn’t distinguish a separation of one position from a separation of ten, for example. So, explicit positional methods are still useful (or even essential), since they supply that information.

If this all sounded confusing, don’t worry, it was more meant as an overview, and we will step through these concepts a bit more slowly in the remaining article.

2. Adding a position vector to each token embedding

We briefly talked about implicit positional information through causal attention masks (and we will revisit this again in more detail later). However, we now switch gears and talk about the explicit ways to add positional information.

The perhaps simplest and most direct (but not “cheapest”) approach is to give each sequence position its own vector. We add that vector to the token embedding, so the resulting, modified embedding vector is then passed to the first transformer block. This exact procedure is discussed in the following subsections.

2.1 Learned absolute position embeddings in GPT-2

To illustrate the use of an explicit, learned positional vector to the input embeddings, let’s consider GPT-2 as a concrete example. GPT-2 learns a table of position vectors during training. The following figure illustrates where these vectors are used in the architecture, that is, immediately after the token embedding lookup and before the first transformer block.

Figure 5: GPT-2 adds absolute position information before the transformer blocks, as highlighted in purple. The dimensions shown are for GPT-2 XL.

The positional embedding layer depicted in the figure above follows the same implementation as the token embedding layer, i.e., they have exactly the same output dimensions so that the resulting input vectors can be added to each other. The code below shows that with an excerpt of a GPT-2 code implementation:

class GPTModel(nn.Module): def __init__(self, cfg): super().__init__() self.tok_emb = nn.Embedding(cfg["vocab_size"], cfg["emb_dim"]) self.pos_emb = nn.Embedding(cfg["context_length"], cfg["emb_dim"]) self.drop_emb = nn.Dropout(cfg["drop_rate"]) self.trf_blocks = nn.Sequential( *[TransformerBlock(cfg) for _ in range(cfg["n_layers"])]) self.final_norm = LayerNorm(cfg["emb_dim"]) self.out_head = nn.Linear( cfg["emb_dim"], cfg["vocab_size"], bias=False ) def forward(self, in_idx): batch_size, seq_len = in_idx.shape tok_embeds = self.tok_emb(in_idx) pos_embeds = self.pos_emb(torch.arange(seq_len, device=in_idx.device)) x = tok_embeds + pos_embeds # Shape [batch_size, num_tokens, emb_size] x = self.drop_emb(x) x = self.trf_blocks(x) x = self.final_norm(x) logits = self.out_head(x) return logits

And in mathematical terms, we can write it as follows. Let m be a position index and xm the token ID at that position. The input to the first block is

Here,

Etoken is the embedding layer,

Pm is the position info vector selected by m,

and hm is the resulting summed embedding and position vector.

The two vectors have the same width, so we add their entries element-wise. Figure 6 below shows this visually with a small numerical example.

Figure 6: Token and position embeddings are added element-wise. (I am following the convention here that neural network diagrams are usually read from bottom to top.)

In the figure above, at position 0, adding the token embedding [1, 1, 1] to the position embedding [1.1, 1.2, 1.3] gives [2.1, 2.2, 2.3]. By the way, the values in the figure are chosen to keep the illustration simple; in a real example, both embedding layers (tables) are initialized with small random values and learned during training.

So, what we covered in this section is a so-called absolute positional method because the positional embedding Pm depends on the token’s own position m. In the previous example sentence, the two “fox” tokens have initially the same token embeddings, but because we are adding different positional vectors P2 and P7, they have different values before they enter the first transformer block. And consequently, the query, key, and value vectors inside the attention mechanisms then also receive representations that already include position information.

Note that the original GPT-2 implementation has 1,024 learned position vectors. This means it only works with inputs with 1024 or fewer tokens. If we want to extend the context window, we would have to extend this embedding table. And since the embedding table (layer) is updated like any other layer during training, the context length would have to be decided before the training starts.

Extending the context would be very expensive since the table can become very large. For example, in the case of GPT-2 Medium, the positional embedding layer weights only make up 0.30% of the total weights at 1,024 positions. However, if we increased the context length to a nowadays common 100k range, they would make up >22% of the total model weights, which would be very uneconomic.

2.2 Fixed sinusoidal position encodings in the original transformer

The original “Attention Is All You Need” transformer from 2017, which predates GPT, uses the same addition mechanism, but computes its position vectors with fixed sine and cosine functions. Figure 7 below shows this addition before both the encoder and decoder stacks. The encoder processes the input sequence, and the decoder generates the output sequence. (The reason why it’s introduced here is that the sine and cosine functions will become relevant again later when discussing RoPE.)

Figure 7: Sinusoidal position encodings added to the token embeddings as highlighted in purple.

The original transformer also scales its token embeddings by √(dmodel) before adding the position vectors, where dmodel is the embedding width.

So, while GPT-2 in the previous section had the formula

the formula used in the original transformer (depicted in Figure 7) is:

This scaling by √(dmodel) is a minor technical detail, but for those who are curious, the motivation behind this scaling factor is not explained in the transformer paper, but as I discussed with colleagues on a Twitter thread many years ago, it’s likely to put the token embeddings and sinusoidal positional encodings on comparable numerical scales at initialization, so the position vectors don’t dominate the sum when we add them.

In the GPT-2 case, this scaling wasn’t necessary because the original GPT-2 code initialized the token embeddings to have an initial standard deviation of 0.02 and the position embeddings to have an initial standard deviation of 0.01 so that they were already roughly on the same scale.

Now, the more interesting point is how the position embeddings Pm are computed.

Concretely, each position vector has the following entries:

As before, m denotes the position. The index i indexes a pair of features within the vector.

Here, a feature is one number in the position vector. For example, let’s assume we have a small vector with 8 values (dmodel = 8) that has entries numbered 0 through 7. (In the original paper this was 512 for the small model and 1024 for the bigger model, instead of 8, but that would be hard to visualize.) We group these into four pairs so that i goes from 0 through 3.

Figure 8: Sine and cosine entries for each feature pair.

Here, the indices 2i and 2i + 1 select the two entries corresponding to pair i.

As shown in the equation earlier, the angle at position m, which is then fed into the sine or cosine functions to obtain Pm, 2i or Pm, 2i + 1, respectively, is computed as follows:

Figure 9: The angle inside sine and cosine is the token position multiplied by the pair’s angular frequency.

Figure 10 shows this calculation for all four pairs of our 8-feature example at position m = 2.

Figure 10: The four sine and cosine pairs at position 2.

For an eight-feature embedding, the four frequencies are 1, 0.1, 0.01, and 0.001 radians per position. At position 2, the first pair in row 1 is (sin 2, cos 2) ≈ (0.909, −0.416).

The second pair, in row 2, changes more slowly, giving (sin 0.2, cos 0.2) ≈ (0.199, 0.980).

The figure below visualizes the sine and cosine calculations for different pairs, which are then collected in a position vector.

Figure 11: Sampling sine and cosine functions to form a position vector.

In short, this method is a (more complicated) alternative to obtaining positional embedding vectors compared to the GPT-2 method discussed earlier.

Similar to the GPT-2 embeddings, these are also absolute embeddings because each vector is computed from its own position. However, in contrast to the GPT-2 approach, the sine and cosine functions stay fixed during training. (Also, they can be extended and evaluated beyond the training positions, like beyond the context length of 1024, although that alone doesn’t guarantee that the model will perform well there, because it hasn’t seen those during training.)

To summarize, Figure 12 below compares the two ways of obtaining a position vector we discussed so far.

Figure 12: Fixed sinusoidal and learned absolute positional embeddings.

This sinusoidal method may look like an overly complicated way to obtain positional embeddings, however, understanding it will be useful when talking about RoPE in the next section.

Also, there is some rationale behind it. The authors chose sine and cosine because they make the shifting by a certain offset gives a certain deterministic difference. For example, the difference between position 2 to 5 and the difference between 102 to 105 is both 3, which then results in the same rotation.

According to the Attention Is All You Need paper:

“...easily learn to attend by relative positions, since for any fixed offset k, PEpos + k can be represented as a linear function of PEpos.”

Or in less technical terms, the authors hypothesized that this property would make it easier for attention to learn relationships such as “three tokens earlier.”

Figure 13 below illustrates this for one sine/cosine pair. A rotation is a linear transformation, and both three-position shifts use the same rotation.

Figure 13: For a pair with angular frequency 0.1 radians per position, a shift of three positions always rotates the pair by 0.3 radians.

3. Encoding relative position with RoPE

So, the sinusoidal method above is still considered an absolute positional encoding because the vector added to each token embedding represents that token’s position counted from the start of the sequence.

As shown in the previous figure, for example, tokens at positions 2 and 5 receive the position vectors P2 and P5. If we move those tokens to positions 102 and 105, they’re still three positions apart. But each token gets a different position vector because its absolute position m has changed.

So their attention score can change even when the token embeddings stay the same. The sinusoidal structure still gives the model a way to learn relative relationships.

Anyways, RoPE, which we will discuss below, builds this relative relationship directly into the attention calculation. RoPE, which Su et al. introduced in the 2021 RoFormer paper, stands for Rotary Position Embedding and is considered a relative position method.

3.1 Moving the rotation inside attention in RoPE

In attention, each token’s current representation is projected into a query, a key, and a value vector. Query-key dot products determine attention scores. After masking and softmax, the resulting weights determine how the value vectors are mixed. For a refresher, see my Understanding and Coding Self-Attention, Multi-Head Attention, Causal-Attention, and Cross-Attention in LLMs article:

Understanding and Coding Self-Attention, Multi-Head Attention, Causal-Attention, and Cross-Attention in LLMs

January 14, 2024

Read full story

RoPE uses the token’s position to rotate its query and key vectors before their dot product. The value vectors bypass this rotation. Figure 14 below shows an overview of where RoPE is applied in a relatively recent MiniMax-M3, where QK-Norm normalizes queries and keys before RoPE.

Figure 14: MiniMax-M3 applies RoPE to queries and keys inside attention.

As shown in the figure above, the LLM architecture has no explicit position vector added alongside the initial token embedding. Its explicit position signal is injected inside attention instead.

Side note: MiniMax-M3 rotates only part of each query and key head, a variation known as “partial RoPE” that we will cover later in Section 6.1. But to understand the basic RoPE operation first, let’s walk through the method step by step using a small example.

3.2 From token representations to feature pairs in RoPE

Attention usually divides its query, key, and value features into multiple heads, which are processed in parallel. Hence, the full mechanism is usually called Multi-head Attention (note that Grouped Query Attention also uses multiple heads). Here, each attention head computes its own attention scores and weighted sums. RoPE then acts on pairs of numbers within each query and key head.

To make this concrete, Figure 12 below follows a token representation through the learned query and key projections, which are computed via the weight matrices WQ and WK. In this toy example, each projection produces 16 features, which are reshaped into two heads with eight features each. In the figure below, every small cell contains one scalar feature, meaning one numerical entry in a vector (the numbers are not shown due to space reasons).

Figure 15: Projecting a hidden state, reshaping into heads, and pairing features for RoPE.

For the first eight-feature head, [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8], we pair each feature in the first half with the corresponding feature in the second half. This gives (1, 5), (2, 6), (3, 7), and (4, 8). This split-half convention is used in LLM implementations, including my Qwen3 implementation. (We will compare it with the original, alternative adjacent-feature pairing method later after implementing the basic operation.)

Each pair gets a rotation angle determined by the token position m and that pair’s frequency θi. The corresponding query and key pairs use the same frequency. Figure 16 below shows how we rotate the pairs, put the updated features back in their original locations, and compute the query-key dot product.

Figure 16: Pairwise rotations produce the rotated query and key vectors used to compute attention scores.

The next section will walk through the figure(s) a bit more slowly.

3.3 Rotating one pair in RoPE

For the following example, we’ll use the same two positions as before. The query comes from “the” at position m = 5, and the key comes from the first “fox” at position n = 2. Since, in this example, the key comes before the query, the causal mask allows the query to attend to it.

To illustrate the effect that the position has, and keeping everything fixed, let’s assume we start both pairs from the same two values, (1, 5).

Figure 17: The first feature pair, (1, 5), takes one entry from each half of the query head.

Furthermore, for this example, let’s use θ = 20° per token position (π/9 radians per position). At position 2, we rotate the key’s arrow counterclockwise by 2 × 20° = 40°. At position 5, we rotate the query’s arrow by 5 × 20° = 100°.

Figure 18: Starting from (1, 5), RoPE rotates the key pair at position 2 by 40° and the query pair at position 5 by 100°.

To illustrate what’s happening in the figure above, for example, plugging cos 40° ≈ 0.766 and sin 40° ≈ 0.643 into the rotation from Figure 16 gives the new key coordinates:

Then, changing 40° to 100°, using the same equation as above, the rotated query pair becomes q′ ≈ (−5.098, 0.117). But the important point is that both arrows still have length √26. Only the rotation changes but the vectors keep the same lengths after the transformation.

So, for a query pair (a, b) at position m, the two operations here are.

For the key pair at position n, we use the same equations with nθ.

(Section 4 later shows how RoPE calculates the angular frequency for each feature pair.)

To close this section, for those more comfortable reading code, everything about RoPE illustrated so far can be written in code, as follows:

import torch query_head = torch.tensor([1., 2., 3., 4., 5., 6., 7., 8.]) key_head = query_head.clone() # Split-half pairing gives (1, 5), (2, 6), (3, 7), and (4, 8). query_pairs = torch.stack(query_head.chunk(2), dim=-1) key_pairs = torch.stack(key_head.chunk(2), dim=-1) # Take the first feature pair from each head. q = query_pairs[0] k = key_pairs[0] m = 5 # Query position (in our example, "the") n = 2 # Key position (in our example, the first "fox") theta = torch.deg2rad(torch.tensor(20.0)) # Radians per token position def rotate_pair(pair, angle): a, b = pair c, s = torch.cos(angle), torch.sin(angle) return torch.stack((a * c - b * s, a * s + b * c)) k_rot = rotate_pair(k, n * theta) # 2 × 20° = 40° q_rot = rotate_pair(q, m * theta) # 5 × 20° = 100° print(f"Original key: ({k[0]:g}, {k[1]:g})") print(f"Original query: ({q[0]:g}, {q[1]:g})") print(f"Rotated key: ({k_rot[0]:.3f}, {k_rot[1]:.3f})") print(f"Rotated query: ({q_rot[0]:.3f}, {q_rot[1]:.3f})")

The resulting values are:

Original key: (1, 5) Original query: (1, 5) Rotated key: (-2.448, 4.473) Rotated query: (-5.098, 0.117)

If we rotate all 4 pairs in the 8-feature query (and key) vector(s), we get the vectors below. (We’ll come back to how we go from rotating one pair to rotating all of them later.)

Original key: (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8) Original query: (1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8) Rotated key: (-2.448, 1.577, 2.951, 3.994, 4.473, 6.125, 7.021, 8.003) Rotated query: (-5.098, 0.928, 2.877, 3.986, 0.117, 6.256, 7.051, 8.007)

3.4 How the relative offset changes the attention score in RoPE

Note that, as shown above, the query and key heads have eight features each, which we previously grouped into 4 pairs with two elements each, like (1, 5), and (2, 6), and so on. For simplicity here we’ll follow just the first pair in each head, (1, 5), formed by taking the entries at indices 0 and 4:

Original key: (1, 5) Original query: (1, 5) Rotated key: (-2.448, 4.473) Rotated query: (-5.098, 0.117)

As shown above, in our example, those entries have values (1, 5) in both heads before the rotation.

Also, as in the code above, let’s use the notation q =(q1, q2) and k =(k1, k2) for these two pairs. To compute their dot product, using the standard dot product formula, we multiply corresponding coordinates and add the products:

Before rotation, the pairs have a dot product of 1 × 1 + 5 × 5 = 26. Using the rounded coordinates from the previous Figure 18 gives the contribution from the first pair after rotation:

We can see why by writing the dot product in terms of the lengths of the two vectors (arrows in Figure 18) and the angle ϕ between them.

In both cases, the vector length is √26. The query (position 5) is rotated by 100° and the key (position 2) is rotated by 40°. The vectors (arrows) start from the same direction, so they end up being 60° apart after rotation. Their exact dot product is 26 × cos(60°) = 13.

Now let’s see what happens if we move just the key from position 2 to position 3, but we keep its starting coordinates (1, 5) fixed. The key’s rotation changes now from 2 × 20° = 40° to 3 × 20° = 60°. The query still rotates by 100°, so the angle between the arrows shrinks to 40° and the dot product changes from 13 to 26 × cos(40°) ≈ 19.92. So, moving the key one position closer changed the dot product even though we started with the same feature values.

Why do these dot products matter? In attention, we sum the contributions from all feature pairs to get the query-key dot products. RoPE can change those dot products and thus the weights that softmax assigns to the value vectors.

In the attention equation above, Q′ and K′ are matrices that contain the rotated queries q′ and keys k′ for one head, V contains the values, and d is the head dimension.

3.5 Why shifting both positions preserves the attention score in RoPE

Now that we illustrated the computation of the dot products, which is at the core of the attention mechanism, let’s return to positions m = 5 and n = 2. If we shift both positions to the right by three positions, we get m = 8 and n = 5. Each rotation gains 60° (the query goes from 100° to 160°, and the key goes from 40° to 100°), however, the angle between the vectors stays the same. The dot product for this feature pair is still 13.

Figure 19: The first pair’s dot product at three different position settings.

The key is that the difference between the rotation angles is

If we keep the original query and key vectors fixed and shift both token positions by the same amount, RoPE adds the same rotation to both vectors. The angle between them stays the same, so their dot product stays the same. Note that Figure 20 below uses smaller angles than the actual example (20° per position) to keep the picture readable.

Figure 20: A shared position shift preserves the relative angle and dot product for fixed query and key features.

The examples above and Figure 20 illustrate the main idea behind RoPE. We use each token’s position to rotate the corresponding query and key in the attention module. In the query-key dot product, what matters is how much one vector rotates relative to the other. For each feature pair, this difference in rotation depends only on the offset between the two tokens.

To illustrate this in the big picture context, in Figure 21, each row collects the scores for one query. If we consider row 5, the query for “the”, and column 2, the key for “fox”, the attention score between those two is computed as the dot product between the two rotated versions of the query and key vector.

Figure 21: The attention scores for one head before softmax. Gray cells mark future keys excluded by the causal mask.

What’s the point, again? Let’s once more consider “The red fox jumps over the gray fox.” If we add 100 tokens (e.g., think system prompt) before the sentence, the first “fox” and the second “the” move from positions 2 and 5 to 102 and 105. However, a prefix of any length shifts both positions equally, so they still stay exactly three positions apart. This means, each vector (arrow) gains the exact same additional rotation, which preserves the angle between them and their dot product (assuming we hold the unrotated query and key vectors fixed; in a real model, a new prefix of 100 tokens can change those vectors by changing what the tokens attend to).

Figure 22 tracks this comparison in the attention score matrix. The right panel shows the rows and columns for the sentence after we add the prefix. In our toy example, the first pair still contributes 13 to the dot product.

Figure 22: The highlighted score moves from row 5, column 2 to row 105, column 102. It keeps the same value if we hold the query and key vectors fixed before rotation.

As you may recall, in the original transformer’s sinusoidal encoding, a fixed offset also corresponds to a fixed rotation within each sine/cosine pair. But that shared rotation applies only to the position vectors (added to the input vector) instead of the key and query vectors inside the attention mechanism.

Figure 23 compares what this shift changes in the three methods we covered so far.

Figure 23: The same position shift under sinusoidal encodings, GPT-2’s learned position embeddings, and RoPE.

3.6 Bonus: Combining the pairs into a complete head in RoPE

To see how the relative offset affects the dot product, we focused on one feature pair in the query and key. Now let’s put all four pairs together and see how RoPE rotates the full eight-feature vectors in one attention head. (Please feel free to skip this section if it is too much technical detail.)

Figure 24 picks up where Figure 17 left off. We rotate each of the four pairs, then put the results back in the query’s original feature order.

Figure 24: RoPE rotates each pair of query features at its own angle. (The same can be applied to the key, which is not shown to keep the illustration simple).

Figure 24 above shows the rotated query q′ (at the bottom). Similarly, we could get the rotated key k′ by repeating the same steps at the key’s position.

Recall the two equations from Section 3.3. For query pair i with input values (ai, bi), let ci = cos(mθi) and si = sin(mθi). These are the cosine and sine of the rotation angle for that pair at query position m:

Figure 25 below shows the same four rotations between the four pairs as a single matrix multiplication, q′ = Rmq.

Figure 25: The four rotations from Figure 24 written as one matrix multiplication. The pair labels show where each rotation goes in the full matrix.

4. Computing rotation frequencies in RoPE?

A full attention head uses multiple frequencies so that some feature pairs change more drastically (”quickly” in RoPE terms) with position and others change more gradually (”slowly” in RoPE terms). This serves the same purpose as the multiple frequencies in the original transformer’s sinusoidal encodings.

However, instead of us assigning these manually, for a head with d features, standard full RoPE computes one frequency for each of its d/2 pairs as follows:

Here,

d is the head dimension (it has to be even so that all features can be arranged in pairs).

i is the index of the pair; it ranges from 0 to d/2−1.

b is a chosen base value (hyperparameter) that determines the spacing of the frequencies.

θi is pair i‘s frequency in radians per token position.

The original RoFormer paper uses b = 10,000, borrowing the original transformer’s frequency formula with head dimension d in place of embedding width dmodel.

For example, with head dimension d = 8 and base b = 10,000, we get frequencies [1, 0.1, 0.01, 0.001] radians per position.

5. Bonus: Implementing RoPE in PyTorch

If you want to see RoPE in a complete model, I recommend checking out my end-to-end Qwen3 from-scratch implementation.

Since code can be an effective medium for explaining and understanding concepts, the following subsection will walk you through the core parts that we have covered so far, if you are interested. Otherwise, please feel free to fast forward to section 6, on Positional choices in modern architectures.

So, for the following code snippets, we’ll use the same split-half rotation convention as mentioned earlier (the interleaved convention will also be covered, no worries). First, we’ll apply it to one query vector, then we’ll use it on queries and keys inside the attention mechanism.

5.1 Computing and applying the rotations

First, let’s reuse the 8-feature query from Figure 24. For convenience, below are its four feature pairs before rotation.

Detail from Figure 24. The eight query features form four pairs in the split-half layout.

In the earlier non-coding example, we chose a frequency of 20° per token position for the first pair to keep the math simple. Here, we’ll use the original RoPE formula from the previous Section 4 above, θi = b−2i/d, with head dimension d = 8 and base b = 10,000.

Figure 26 shows how the two functions work together to apply these rotations.

Figure 26: rope_params returns the cosine and sine tables for token position 2. rope_split_half takes those tables and the original query and returns the rotated query.

As shown in the figure above, first, rope_params determines how far to rotate each pair, returning two “tables” that store the cosine and sine of those angles. Or, in other words, rope_params computes the cosine and sine of each rotation angle, which depends on the token’s position and the feature pair’s frequency.

import torch def rope_params(head_dim, positions, base=10_000): assert head_dim % 2 == 0 indices = torch.arange(0, head_dim, 2, device=positions.device) inv_freq = base ** (-indices.float() / head_dim) angles = positions.float()[:, None] * inv_freq[None, :] return angles.cos(), angles.sin()

The indices array in the rope_params function contains the 2i values from the frequency formula in Section 4:

For our eight-feature query, the indicesare [0, 2, 4, 6], that is, one 2i value for each feature pair. Suppose we use the common base b = 10,000. And following our earlier examples, we choose a small head dimension of d = 8. In this case, the inv_freq then computes the four pair frequencies θi = b−2i/d as follows:

As we can see above, the resulting inv_freq has one frequency θi for each of the four feature pairs ( measured in radians) per token position.

Next, the angles then multiplies each pair’s frequency θi with the token position m, which computes the rotation angle mθi. In our example, with positions = [2], we get:

Finally, rope_params takes the cosine and sine of each angle and outputs them as two so-called cosine and sine tables, cos and sin. In both tables, each row is a token position and each column is a feature pair. Since we only use token position 2 here, each table has shape (1, 4), with the single row shown below:

For the example in Figure 26, each table has one row for token position 2 and four columns, one for each feature pair. Figure 27 shows the rotation angle for each pair and the corresponding entries in the two tables.

Figure 27: The two tables returned by rope_params for token position 2. The highlighted column gives the cosine and sine used to rotate the first feature pair, (1, 5).

In short, rope_params computes the cosine and sine of each rotation angle, which depends on the token’s position and the feature pair’s frequency.

In a real-world scenario, if we pass rope_params 1,024 token positions for a context size of 1024, and a head dimension of 128, it returns two tensors, cos and sin, each with shape (1024, 64) since a head with 128 features has 64 pairs.

The tables have no batch or head dimension, and we broadcast each (1024, 64) table across the batch and head dimensions. So, if we have multi-head attention or grouped query attention with 32 heads, feature pair 0 at token position 2 uses the same rotation angle in all 32 heads. Each head rotates its own feature values, so the rotated queries can still differ across heads. Also, the same entries can be reused across queries, keys, and heads that use the same positions and frequencies. In short, we only need to (pre)compute these tables once per model for all positions up to a maximum supported context size.

Technically, it would also be possible to store these tables alongside the model weights; but they are so incredibly cheap to compute that storing them on disk would be overkill.

Next, rope_split_half uses those cosines and sines to rotate the actual features in the query and key vectors inside attention.

def rope_split_half(x, cos, sin): first, second = x.float().chunk(2, dim=-1) return torch.cat(( first * cos - second * sin, first * sin + second * cos, ), dim=-1).to(x.dtype)

Considering the simple 8-feature example once more, the .chunk in the code snippet above splits the query into [1, 2, 3, 4] and [5, 6, 7, 8] and lines up the two entries in each pair. For example, the first slot of each half gives us (1, 5), the second (2, 6), and so on. The two expressions inside torch.cat compute the rotated halves using the equations from Section 3.3:

Note that here, everything denoted with ai corresponds to the first half of the query, and everything denoted with bi comes from the second half. The first expression inside torch.cat computes all the ai′values, and the second computes all the bi′ values.

Then, we concatenate the halves, which puts each value back in its original index position via torch.cat.

Here’s the code that produces the rotated query in the earlier Figure 26. The shown reshape (1, 1, 1, 8) just brings the simple toy array into the expected format: one batch item, one head, one token, and eight features.

positions = torch.tensor([2]) cos, sin = rope_params(8, positions) q = torch.arange(1., 9.).reshape(1, 1, 1, 8) q_rot = rope_split_half(q, cos, sin) print(q_rot[0, 0, 0])

With several positions, each token simply picks up its own row from the cos and sin tables. Through broadcasting, PyTorch reuses these same tables across all batch items and attention heads.

5.2 Using RoPE inside causal attention

Europe’s Wadden Sea is a natural wonder. So why might it join the world heritage in danger list?

Europe’s Wadden Sea is a natural wonder. So why might it join the world heritage in danger list?. Along the coasts of the Netherlands, Germany and Denmark, its intertidal flats host a wealth of marine life and birds. But extraction industries, tourism and fishing are taking their toll Stand on the coastline at high tide and you look out over unbroken water, with sandy islands rising a few miles offshore. A few hours later and the view across the Wadden Sea has transformed into a maze of deep channels, drying sandbanks, salt marshes and mud flats. As the largest unbroken system of intertidal sand and mud flats in the world , this ever-changing habitat stretches hundreds of miles along the north European coasts of Germany, Denmark and the Netherlands and was awarded world heritage status by Unesco in 2009. Aerial view of the unique features of the Wadden Sea coast. Photograph: Janis Meyer/CWSS Continue reading.

Benchmark rate for banks' mortgage loans continues to rise

Benchmark rate for banks' mortgage loans continues to rise. The benchmark rate for banks' mortgage rates increased for a second consecutive month in October in line with high market rates, data showed Monday. COFIX, or the Cost of Funds Index, a benchmark lending rate for new mortgage loans, came to 3.62 percent last month, up 0.19 percentage point from the previous month, according to the data from the Korea Federation of Banks. The rate was the highest since February 2024, when the corresponding reading was 3.62 percent. COFIX is calculated based on the funding costs of eight domestic banks in Korea. They include Nonghyup Bank, Shinhan Bank, Woori Bank and Citibank Korea. The Bank of Korea (BOK) kept its benchmark interest rate unchanged last month at 3 percent after two consecutive rate hikes to give the central bank time to assess economic conditions amid prolonged tensions in the Middle East and robust exports. The rate freeze came after the BOK raised the benchmark rate in its July and August meetings, citing "preemptive action" to curb mounting inflationary pressure and ensure macroeconomic stability.

The country's bank branch network has continued to shrink, falling to 5,466 locations at the end of September from 5,831 at the end of 2022, according to the FSS. Of these, only 991 offered services outside standard banking hours. Access to staffed branches with extended hours is particularly limited. About 40.3 percent of these locations are in the Seoul metropolitan area, while only 10.6 percent open on weekends. LG유플러스, 네트워크·보안 통합 승부수… ‘U+SASE’로 기업 보안시장 공략. 클라우드와 생성형 인공지능(AI) 확산으로 기업의 업무 환경이 빠르게 분산되면서 네트워크와 보안을 하나의 체계로 관리하려는 수요가 커지고 있다. 통신망과 보안 솔루션을 별도로 운영하던 기존 방식에서 벗어나 네트워크 단계에서부터 보안을 통합하는 시장이 확대될 것으로 전망되는 가운데 LG유플러스가 기업용 보안 플랫폼 시장 공략에 속도를 낸다.LG유플러스는 기업의 네트워크와 보안 기능을 클라우드 기반으로 통합 관리할 수 있는 ‘U+SASE’를 정식 출시했다.U+SASE는 본사와 지사를 비롯해 데이터센터, 클라우드, 재택·원격근무 환경 등 기업의 업무 공간에서 발생하는 네트워크 연결과 보안 기능을 하나의 플랫폼에서 관리하는 서비스다.최근 기업 업무 환경이 클라우드와 서비스형 소프트웨어(Saa S) 중심으로 전환되면서 기존 사내 네트워크를 중심으로 구축된 보안 체계만으로는 대응이 어려워지고 있다. 특히 원격근무와 생성형 AI 활용이 늘면서 기업 내부 데이터가 외부 서비스와 연결되는 접점도 확대

How to disable 5 hidden location trackers killing your iPhone’s battery

How to disable 5 hidden location trackers killing your i Phone’s battery. Do you always feel like somebody’s watching you? . You probably know your mapping app needs your location to give you directions. But your i Phone may be letting dozens of other apps ping your exact GPS coordinates in the background, draining your battery and quietly building a profile of everywhere you go. Retail apps want to know when you walk into their competitors’ stores. Weather widgets constantly refresh your coordinates on your home screen even when you haven’t tapped them in a week. All of this silent radio activity burns through your battery before dinner time. You don’t have to put your i Phone in airplane mode to stop it. You just need to shut down the unnecessary trackers hiding deep inside your system settings. Kill precise location for apps that don’t need it Your weather app needs to know what city you’re in, but it doesn’t need to know what side of the street you’re standing on. Go to Settings > Privacy & Security > Location Services. Scroll through your apps and look for anything that isn’t a map or ride-sharing service. Tap each app and toggle off Precise Location at the bottom of the screen. Restrict access to ‘While Using’ While you’re in that same menu, make sure no app is set to Always. Change every app’s permission to While Using the App. This ensures a fast-food app or social media network can’t track your morning commute while your i Phone is locked in your pocket. Shut down hidden Apple system services This is where the real battery drains hide. Scroll to the very bottom of the Location Services menu and tap System Services. You’ll see a massive list of internal trackers. You can safely turn off Location-Based Apple Ads, Routing & Traffic, and i Phone Analytics. Your i Phone will still work perfectly, but it won’t constantly log your daily routine to send back to Cupertino. Delete your ‘Significant Locations’ history Inside that same System Services menu, look for a tab called Significant Locations. Apple uses this to track the places you visit most often to provide predictive traffic routing. If you authenticate and open it, you will likely see a deeply detailed, borderline creepy map of your daily life. Tap Clear History at the bottom, then toggle the feature completely off at the top. Turn off background app refresh Apps love to use your location to download fresh data while you aren’t looking. Go to Settings > General > Background App Refresh. You don’t need to turn the master switch off completely, but you should absolutely go down the list and toggle it off for social media, retail, and gaming apps that have no business running in the background. .
RSS · 15 Nov 2026, 09:00

The chip war never ended

The chip war never ended. It’s no secret that the world runs on computer chips. This became clear during the pandemic, when a race for even basic chips needed to operate cars and other devices set back manufacturing timelines across the world. Beltway policymakers are reminded of just how important semiconductors are to the world every time China seems to move toward invading Taiwan, which raises the specter that the country could subsume control of TSCM, the most prominent computer chip contract manufacturer. Now Jensen Huang’s exit from Nvidia , which comes after reports of at least three known extramarital affairs—one of them with an alleged spy for the Chinese government—has thrown the world’s supply of computer chips back in the spotlight. With stocks falling and no obvious succession plan, it’s not clear what the future of Nvidia is right now, or how it might jostle the broader semiconductor ecosystem. “Many companies that have in the past relied on Nvidia will be watching very carefully to see whether they can rely on Nvidia going forward, and if not, they might turn to competitors,” says Chris Miller, a Tufts University professor and author of the 2022 book Chip War , which documents how the world came to depend on these tiny components. Fast Company chatted with Miller on November 12 about Washington, D.C.’s response to the Huang scandal, Nvidia’s future, and the prospect of keeping advanced semiconductors out of countries like China. This interview has been edited for length and clarity. You’ve followed the chip industry for quite a long time. Are you surprised by what’s happened over the last few weeks? . Jensen was an extraordinary CEO for 33 years, since Nvidia’s founding. [He] deserves a lot of the credit for the company’s extraordinary rise, but also deserves a lot of the blame for a series of poor decisions that have led not just to his ouster, but to a period of crisis for the company. Jensen really was central to Nvidia’s success, and he ran the company in a pretty unique way. He had 60 different people reporting to him directly, so there wasn’t a sort of standard corporate hierarchy, which worked really well for a long time. But it means that now that he’s gone, it’s not obvious who can fill his shoes. There’s not really a deputy who was waiting in the wings to take over. I’m curious about the history of espionage attempts like what’s being alleged here. There is some history during the Cold War, for example. There were efforts by spies from the Soviet Union or Eastern European countries to get access to sensitive technology by getting close to semiconductor executives. But I think the scale of this is kind of unique. First, that it happened to such a central company in the U.S. tech ecosystem, the most valuable company in the world. That’s somewhat unique. The second thing that’s unique is that the affair in question was both with a major customer, which shouldn’t have happened, as well as an [alleged] Chinese intelligence.

DOCKET: A Pluralistic Benchmark for Evaluating AI-Assisted Policy Feedback in U.S. Rulemaking

DOCKET: A Pluralistic Benchmark for Evaluating AI-Assisted Policy Feedback in U.S. Rulemaking. ar Xiv:2611.17914v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: As AI models advance and impact deliberative democracies, there is a growing need to assess their support of pluralistic viewpoints. Most pluralism benchmarks are insufficient for this: they are not grounded in diversity (i.e., stakeholders across different frames, skills and needs), quality (i.e., rigorous and technical debates), and impact (i.e., invested deliberators and real-world outcomes). To address this, we first (i) curate DOCKET, the most comprehensive dataset of the U.S. Federal Notice & Comment process to date. DOCKET consists of 42.3M public comments on 22,400 regulations across 44 government agencies between 2016 and 2026 and contains millions of policy debates; we link public comments to particular policy items proposed by regulators to track the public's attention and the responsiveness of regulators to public input. (ii) Next, we demonstrate the pluralistic utility of DOCKET - we show that public commenters have a wide breadth of perspectives with significant depth in policy expertise and that regulators are often responsive. (iii) Finally, we present DOCKETBench, the first systematic evaluation of LLMs' ability to represent human policy perspectives in federal rulemaking. Only 18-30% of LLM-generated feedback aligns with real human comments. LLMs critique proposals broadly but generally, lacking the specific grounding, citations, and lived-experience arguments that humans bring to bear; in addition, most models we test systematically agree with government perspectives (overlapping with up to 68% of their claims) and agree least with labor unions (as little as 26% of their claims), demonstrating a fundamental skew in perspectives that even tools like persona modeling do not solve. Taken together, we reveal fundamental gaps in how language models navigate real policy issues, and DOCKETBench serves as a testbed for closing those gaps in a crucial real-world domain.
RSS · 16 Nov 2026, 06:00

Large language models put existential risk from artificial intelligence at 10%

Large language models put existential risk from artificial intelligence at 10%. ar Xiv:2611.18219v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: How likely is it that advanced artificial intelligence (AI) will cause human extinction or a similarly permanent disempowerment of humanity? Humans, including AI experts, frequently disagree on this question. Yet one important perspective is missing from this debate: that of AI systems themselves. We put the question to 241 large language models (LLMs) at every available reasoning setting. The models converged on a median risk of 10.0%, with the middle 90% of their estimates between 3.5% and 15.8%, far narrower than the spread among human experts. More expensive, and typically more capable, models stated higher risks; newer models showed more uniform, but not higher, risks. More reasoning barely changed the models' estimates but widened their uncertainty, mostly toward higher risk. Almost all models named loss of control over AI as the most likely cause. These answers are not independent judgments; they likely echo training data, including published expert polls, and choices made in aligning the models. Still, they document the position that current AI systems state on the risks of their own technology. Whether this position reflects any genuine understanding of the systems' own capabilities remains open. Repeated measurements can show whether it shifts as AI systems advance, and how training and alignment shape it is a question for both research and public debate.
RSS · 16 Nov 2026, 06:00

Between Obligations and Evidence: A Participatory Study of Translating the EU AI Act into Technical Requirements

Between Obligations and Evidence: A Participatory Study of Translating the EU AI Act into Technical Requirements. ar Xiv:2611.18439v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: The EU AI Act imposes technical and organizational requirements for high-risk AI systems, but applying these requirements involves contextual choices about what to measure and what evidence supports a compliance claim. We investigate these challenges through a participatory workshop involving 32 researchers, policymakers, and practitioners from different disciplines, using realistic scenarios in healthcare, recruitment, and public transport to examine how regulatory obligations are interpreted, translated into technical requirements, and evaluated in practice. Through a thematic analysis of workshop data, we identify recurring tensions between how regulatory obligations are translated into measurable system properties, how responsibility and human oversight are exercised in practice, and how the validity of compliance evidence is affected by changing operational conditions and system uses. For example, participants highlighted how fairness metrics could conceal discrimination in recruitment, how time pressure and limited information could undermine human oversight in public transport, and how changes in patient populations could invalidate previously established evidence of system performance. Our findings suggest that technical, organizational, and evidentiary challenges are interdependent, and that establishing and maintaining compliance requires attention not only to technical assessments but also to how AI systems are used, governed, and monitored over time. We discuss implications for supporting context-sensitive and ongoing compliance.

Measured from the feed: the unsustainable growth of AI video generation

Measured from the feed: the unsustainable growth of AI video generation. ar Xiv:2611.17938v1 Announce Type: cross Abstract: Text-to-video generation runs at commercial scale, but its broader environmental impacts remain, to our knowledge, understudied. Providers rarely disclose video volumes or energy needs; we measured the yearly video output of two commercial platforms and built a prospective model of such services' absolute footprint from training to storage. Our projections suggest that reducing emissions depends on both limiting growth in video generation and achieving substantial efficiency gains. Under continued growth, emissions rise nearly seventy-five-fold by 2030 despite technological improvements. When usage remains at 2026 levels, the same improvements reduce emissions by about two-fifths. These estimates are a conservative lower other services and uses would push the total higher.

Agent Security Meets Regulatory Reality -- A Practitioner Systematization of Autonomous-Agent Threats and Controls in Regulated Financial Systems

Agent Security Meets Regulatory Reality -- A Practitioner Systematization of Autonomous-Agent Threats and Controls in Regulated Financial Systems. ar Xiv:2606.29142v2 Announce Type: replace Abstract: Large language model agents are entering regulated financial systems, yet research on their attack surface remains predominantly laboratory-based, and practitioner guidance is neither peer-reviewed nor connected to a formal threat model. Using production evidence, we map six agentic threat categories, namely prompt injection, identity and authorization, action auditability, tool abuse, data residency, and boundary policy enforcement, to the specific control obligations imposed by US and EU financial regulation (ECOA and Regulation B, EU AI Act, GDPR Article 22, and FINRA's 2026 agent guidance), showing how legal accountability increases their operational significance. We distill these obligations into five runtime control invariants with reference algorithms that enforce them at auditable architectural boundaries. We develop a formal model expressing each invariant as a predicate over a case-bound event chain and prove the audit reconstruction gate is sound and complete with respect to these invariants: compliance is decidable from the case identifier alone. We show that two of the invariants constrain audit cost and throughput rather than constituting pure operational overhead. We document four architectural patterns from a production Know Your Customer (KYC) deployment for a consumer credit product that reduced multi-day manual processing to same-day automated resolution for approximately four in five cases. Finally, we report three negative results, two identified through internal audit or production observation rather than adversarial testing. We conclude that securing agents in regulated environments depends less on novel attack classes than on rendering auditability, least-privilege authorization, and boundary policy enforcement, with verifiable compliance and bounded audit cost at scale.

AI-Generated Books Confront Publishing Industry With Big Questions

AI-Generated Books Confront Publishing Industry With Big Questions. Romance novelist Coral Hart publishes her own romance novels for the Kindle, and found a kind of superpowere when she realized "the algorithm favours those who can produce a new book at least once a month," reports The Guardian: Hart says she knows of authors who hire more than a dozen ghostwriters to keep pace. By herself, Hart can write 10,000 words a day, but AI could help her produce many more. Since 2025 she has written 370 novels under 21 pen names using AI, earning herself "high six figures". Like any Kindle Unlimited author, she is only paid per pages read: it would make no business sense for her to churn out books that readers quickly abandon. "So, it's not taking the human out of the equation. It's taking the friction and the time and the business blocks out of the way." One study (awaiting peer review), led by Tuhin Chakrabarty of Stony Brook University, estimates that around one fifth of recently self-published books contain at least 25% AI-generated text. Hart now teaches courses on how to use AI to produce books. Unless disclosing the use of AI becomes a legal requirement, she advises other authors not to do so because of the public response. "The minute someone discloses [AI use] they get death threats, they get called names, they're ousted," she says. In the literary world, many people fear the tech will undercut and replace them. "I think people are very afraid of AI, and people in publishing are delusional in thinking that it's not going to overtake our industry, that their screaming on social media is going to stop it." But the anger comes from another place, too. If large language models can spit out books that people want to read, or produce a convincing pastiche of your favourite writer, that's almost certainly because they were trained on established writers' stolen work. Many writers oppose AI for both moral and aesthetic reasons. One group of 70 prominent authors wrote an open letter calling on the big five publishers — Penguin Random House, Hachette, Harper Collins, Simon & Schuster and Macmillan — to promise that they will "never release books that were created by a machine". The publishers did not promise. [Tess Callero, a literary agent with Europa Content, says] ". you have to wonder the reason they aren't. Is it because they see a future in which potentially these books could be monetised?"] So where do we go from here? Will big name writers win the argument for keeping AI out of literature, or will our bookshelves and e-readers soon be crammed full of AI-generated stories? And if a reader can't distinguish human from machine writing, does it still matter? The Guardian's author notes this new technology brings new challenges to the book publishing world: Publishers have concerns about US copyright law, "which specifies that a book must be 'human authored' and considers AI-generated text public domain. " The article notes high-profile books being.

Huawei Set To Challenge Tesla Powerwall with New, 20 kW Home Battery

Huawei Set To Challenge Tesla Powerwall with New, 20 k W Home Battery. "Huawei believes its new Luterra S2 is more than just a home backup battery," reports Electrek: This high-tech Huawei home battery pack hopes to hold back Tesla's latest Powerwall with silicon carbide technology, AI-powered energy management baked in, and enough modular storage to keep even large homes bright and warm when the rest of the block goes dark. The Chinese tech firm is calling it an "all-in-one residential energy system" that combines a hybrid inverter, modular battery storage, and AI-powered energy management in a single, simple, and stackable package that's available in single-phase versions that put out between 3 and 10 k W and three-phase versions that can push up to 20 k W. The Luterra S2 is the first system of its kind to use Huawei's proprietary full-silicon carbide (Si C) architecture, which promises improved efficiency, power density, and ease of use. It's so easy, in fact, that Huawei claims it can be installed and commissioned in just minutes. Huawei's basic Luterra S2 configuration starts with "just" 5 k Wh of battery energy storage and 3 k W of output. That's enough to keep essential household appliances, lighting, and electronics running during a power outage, but you'll want to keep going if you're preparing for more than a day or two without power. If that sounds like you, Luterra's got you covered. The modular system can be expanded with modular 7 k Wh LFP battery packs and can keep scaling up to a massive 336 k Wh of total storage across interconnected systems, with inverter output peaking at an impressive 60 k W using three 20 k W units in parallel. Keeping all that power cool and quiet is a Huawei-developed passive cooling system that does without external fans, keeping operating noise levels below a claimed 29 d B (about the same as my Mac mini). of this story at Slashdot. .

Bill Gates Has Earned Billions Investing in Climate-Polluting Landfills, Reports ProPublica

Bill Gates Has Earned Billions Investing in Climate-Polluting Landfills, Reports Pro Publica. Environmental activist Bill Gates has "quietly accumulated a $29 billion stake in America's No. 2 landfill operator, Republic Services," reports Pro Publica, in a new 5,000-word investigation. But inspectors from America's Environmental Protection Agency "have repeatedly found methane — a highly potent greenhouse gas — escaping from Republic landfills at levels above federal limits. Republic has lobbied against regulations that could make the waste industry cleaner, including by keeping organic garbage out of landfills in the first place." While Gates has invested at least $2 billion in green companies, he's "put more than double that amount into a very dirty, old-school industry," their article argues. And Gates has increased his stake in Republic "to 38% of the company, a position worth $29 billion." While fossil fuels get most of the attention, scientists increasingly see landfills as a crucial climate issue. They're responsible for as much as a fifth of the country's emissions of methane, a highly potent greenhouse gas, studies show. Republic alone generates as much climate pollution as 3 million gasoline-burning cars each year, according to its own reports. [Gates] has failed to make Republic live up to his own climate principles, a Pro Publica investigation has found. Federal inspectors have repeatedly found methane escaping from Republic landfills at levels above federal limits. The company has refrained from adopting the ambitious emissions-reduction goal embraced by its main competitor. "It seems very likely that Bill Gates has the power to influence Republic's behavior to make it more climate-friendly, even if that costs the company profits," said Ann Lipton, a legal scholar who studies corporate governance at the University of Colorado, Boulder. Gates didn't respond to repeated requests to discuss his landfill investments, and Pro Publica couldn't find any public comments he's made about them. But the bet has proven wildly lucrative. Since November 1999, when he emerged as the owner of 5% of Republic's shares — "It's 'Garbage In' for Gates," the New York Post headline read — the company's stock price has soared thirtyfold. And Gates has personally received $2.6 billion in dividends, a Pro Publica analysis of securities filings shows. A rare clue to why Gates made his foray into landfills can be found in an interview he gave to Fortune [in 1999]. To fund his plan to give away virtually his entire fortune, he said he'd given his personal money manager, Michael Larson, a simple remit: Sell Microsoft shares, buy safe, boring assets. After that initial investment, Larson progressively increased Gates' stake in Republic over the years that followed. [On behalf of the Gates Foundation] Larson would also acquire a small chunk of Republic's main competitor, Waste Management.
